is asymptotically normal conditional on
a_{\varepsilon,j}
, as
n_{j}
diverges to infinity. This is because
\left(\frac{1}{\sqrt{n_{j}}}\sum_{i\in I_{n,j}}Z_{i,j}\left(\sum_{k=1}^{d_{z}}Z_{i,j,k}^{2}\right),\left[\frac{1}{\sqrt{n_{j}}}\sum_{i\in I_{n,j}}Z_{i,j}\right]\left[\frac{1}{n_{j}}\sum_{i\in I_{n,j}}\left(\sum_{k=1}^{d_{z}}Z_{i,j,k}^{2}\right)\right],\frac{1}{\sqrt{n_{j}}}\sum_{i\in I_{n,j}}Z_{i,j}\left(\sum_{k=1}^{d_{z}}Z_{i,j,k}^{2}\right)\varepsilon_{i,j}\right)
are jointly asymptotically normal given
(a_{\varepsilon,j})_{j\in[J]}
. Then, we have

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\sqrt{n_{j}}}\sum_{i\in I_{n,j}}\widetilde{Z}_{i,j}\sigma(Z_{i,j})(a_{\varepsilon,j}+\varepsilon_{i,j})

\displaystyle=\frac{a_{\varepsilon,j}}{\sqrt{n_{j}}}\sum_{i\in I_{n,j}}Z_{i,j}\left(\sum_{k=1}^{d_{z}}Z_{i,j,k}^{2}\right)+\frac{1}{\sqrt{n_{j}}}\sum_{i\in I_{n,j}}Z_{i,j}\left(\sum_{k=1}^{d_{z}}Z_{i,j,k}^{2}\right)\varepsilon_{i,j}

\displaystyle-\left[\frac{1}{\sqrt{n_{j}}}\sum_{i\in I_{n,j}}Z_{i,j}\right]\left[\frac{a_{\varepsilon,j}}{n_{j}}\sum_{i\in I_{n,j}}\left(\sum_{k=1}^{d_{z}}Z_{i,j,k}^{2}\right)\right]+o_{P}(1),

which is asymptotically normal given
(a_{\varepsilon,j})_{j\in[J]}
as the cluster size diverges to infinity, so that Assumption
1
(ii) is satisfied.

The number of IVs is set to be
d_{z}\in\{1,3\}
,
and
\rho_{\varepsilon v}\in\{0.3,0.5,0.7\}
corresponds to the degree of endogeneity.
In addition, we introduce cluster heterogeneity to the
d_{z}\times 1
first-stage coefficients
\Pi_{z,j}
by letting
\Pi_{z,j}=(\Pi/2,...,\Pi/2)^{\top}
for
1\leq j\leq J/3
,
\Pi_{z,j}=(\Pi,...,\Pi)^{\top}
for
J/3<j\leq 2J/3
,
and
\Pi_{z,j}=(2\Pi,...,2\Pi)^{\top}
for
2J/3<j\leq J
,
where
\Pi\in\{0.25,0.375,0.5\}
.
Such a DGP satisfies our assumptions and allows for heterogeneity in both cluster sizes and IV strengths.
The values of
\beta
and
\gamma
are both set to
1
.
In Section
22
in the Online Supplement, we further report the simulation results for the case where
\Pi
remains the same across clusters, so that the cluster-level heterogeneity in identification strength originates solely from the heterogeneity in cluster size. We find that the overall patterns remain very similar to those reported in Section
7.3
below.

DGP 2.
We consider the linear-in-mean social interaction model detailed in Example
3.3
. Specifically, following
Leung 2022
, we generate the network
\mathcal{A}
with
n=500
nodes as
\mathcal{A}_{i,j}=1\{||\eta_{i}-\eta_{j}||_{2}\geq(7/(\pi n))^{1/2}\}
, where
\eta_{i}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle i.i.d.}}{{\sim}}\text{Uniform}[0,1]^{2}
. Following
Bramoullé et al. 2009
, we generate data according to (
17
) in which
\varepsilon_{i}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle i.i.d.}}{{\sim}}\mathcal{N}(0,0.1)
,

\displaystyle B_{i}=\begin{cases}0&\text{ with probability $1/2$}\\
\exp(-\log(2)+(\log(4))^{1/2}\varepsilon_{i}^{\prime})&\text{ with probability $1/2$}\end{cases},

where
\varepsilon_{i}^{\prime}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle i.i.d.}}{{\sim}}\mathcal{N}(0,1)
and
(\varepsilon_{i},\varepsilon_{i}^{\prime},\eta_{i})
are independent. The observables
(y_{i},X_{i},W_{i},Z_{i})
are as defined in Example
3.6
with
d_{z}=2
.
According the calibration study by
Bramoullé et al. 2009
, we set the parameters
(\alpha,\beta,\delta)=(0.7683,0.4666,0.1507)
. Because the IV strength scales with
\gamma\beta+\delta
, we study the performance of our method under various identification strength by setting this value to be
(0.11,0.1498,0.1896)
,
which correspond to values

(-0.0872,-0.0019,0.0834)

for
\gamma
. For comparison, we note that
Bramoullé et al. 2009
set
\gamma=0.0834
. We follow the classification procedure proposed by
Leung 2022
to obtain the clusters.

1.

Input: a positive integer
L
and network
\mathcal{A}
.

2.

Compute all separated components (no links between two components) of the network denoted as
\{\mathcal{V}_{h}\}_{h\in\{0\}\cup[H]}
, where
\{\mathcal{V}_{h}\}_{h\in\{0\}\cup[H]}
is a partition of
[n]
(n vertexes) and they are sorted in ascending order according to their sizes. We keep all the components whose sizes are greater than 5. Denote the number of components left as
L^{\prime}+1
for some
L^{\prime}\geq 0
.

3.

Suppose the biggest component
\mathcal{V}_{0}
has size
\tilde{n}_{0}
. By permuting labels, we suppose
\mathcal{V}_{0}=[\tilde{n}_{0}]
and denote its adjacency matrix as
\mathcal{A}_{0}
. Then, we compute the graph Laplacian as

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{0}=I_{\tilde{n}_{0}}-D_{0}^{-1/2}\mathcal{A}_{0}D_{0}^{-1/2},

where
D_{0}=\text{diag}(\sum_{i\in[\tilde{n}_{0}]}A_{0,1,i},\cdots,\sum_{i\in[\tilde{n}_{0}]}A_{0,\tilde{n}_{0},i})
is an
\tilde{n}_{0}\times\tilde{n}_{0}
diagonal matrix of degrees.

4.

Obtain the top
L
eigenvector matrix of
\mathcal{L}_{0}
corresponding to its
L
largest eigenvalues and denote it as
V=[V_{1}^{\top},\cdots,V_{\tilde{n}_{0}}^{\top}]^{\top},

where
V_{i}\in\Re^{L}
for
i\in[\tilde{n}_{0}]
.

5.

Apply K-means algorithm to
V
and divide
\mathcal{V}_{0}=[\tilde{n}_{0}]
into
L
groups, denoted as
\mathcal{V}_{0,1},\cdots,\mathcal{V}_{0,L}
.

6.

Output: we obtain
J=L+L^{\prime}
clusters
(\mathcal{V}_{0,1},\cdots,\mathcal{V}_{0,L},\mathcal{V}_{1},\cdots,\mathcal{V}_{L^{\prime}})
.

We set
n=500
and let
L
be
[5,10,20,30]
. The number of clusters (
J
) depends on
L^{\prime}
which varies across simulation replications. We report the average
J
below.

7.2
Inference Methods

We investigate the finite sample performance of the following inference methods.

1.

IM: This inference method is proposed by
Ibragimov and
Müller 2010
, which compares their group-based
t
-test statistic with the critical value of a
t
-distribution with
J-1
degrees of freedom. The test statistic is constructed by separately running the IV regression using the samples in each cluster.

2.

CRS: The approximate randomization test proposed by
Canay
et al. 2017
, which compares IM’s test statistic with the critical value of the sign changes-based randomization distribution of the statistic.

3.

ASY: Compare
T_{CR,n}
, the Wald statistic studentized by CRVE as described in Section
2.3.1
, with a standard normal critical value (as
r=1
here).
This is the standard Wald test.

4.

BCH: This inference method is proposed by
Bester
et al. 2011
, which compares
T_{CR,n}
with
\sqrt{\frac{J}{J-1}}
times
the critical value of a
t
-distribution with
J-1
degrees of freedom.

5.

WRE: Compare the studentized Wald statistic
T_{CR,n}
with the critical value generated by the WRE cluster (WREC) bootstrap procedure, as described in
Davidson and
MacKinnon 2010
,
Finlay and
Magnusson 2019
,
Roodman
et al. 2019
, and
MacKinnon 2023
.

6.

W-B: Compare the unstudentized Wald statistic
T_{n}
, which sets the weighting matrix
\hat{A}_{r}=1
in (
4
), with the wild bootstrap critical value
\hat{c}_{n}
as described in Section
2.3.1
. Therefore, this bootstrap procedure does not involve the CRVE-based studentization.

7.

W-B-S: Compare the studentized Wald statistic
T_{CR,n}
with the wild bootstrap critical value
\hat{c}_{CR,n}
as described in Section
2.3.1
. Its pseudo code is provided in Section
10
in the Online Supplement.

8.

AR-ASY: Compare
AR_{CR,n}
with
\chi^{2}(d_{z})
critical value,
where
AR_{CR,n}
is the AR statistic studentized by CRVE and
d_{z}
is the number of instruments.

9.

AR-B: Compare the unstudentized AR statistic
AR_{n}
, which sets the weighting matrix
\hat{A}_{z}=I_{d_{z}}
in (
12
), with the wild bootstrap critical value
\hat{c}_{AR,n}
as described in Section
2.3.2
.

10.

AR-B-S: Compare
AR_{CR,n}
with the wild bootstrap critical value
\hat{c}_{AR,CR,n}
as described in Section
2.3.2
.

Except for the methods AR-ASY, AR-B, and AR-B-S, all other methods require the estimation of parameter
\beta
. All four k-class estimators mentioned in the paper can be used. Here, we report the results regarding the TSLS estimator for simulations with one IV and report those regarding the FULL estimator for simulations with multiple IVs, as it is known that FULL has reduced finite sample bias than TSLS in the over-identified case.
26
26

26

The performance of LIML is very similar to FULL in our simulations. We therefore omit the LIML results but they are available upon request.

7.3
Simulation Results

DGP 1.
Tables
1
-
2
report the null empirical rejection frequencies of the ten inference methods described above with
J=6
or
12
. For succinctness, we report the results for
J=9
or
20
in Section
22.1
of the Online Supplement.
Also as mentioned above, the results of the Wald tests are based on TSLS when
d_{z}=1
and based on FULL
when
d_{z}=3
, respectively. Following the recommendation in the literature, we set the tuning parameter of FULL to be 1.
27
27

27

In this case FULL is best unbiased to a second order among
k
-class estimators under normal errors
(
Rothenberg 1984
)
.
Several observations are in order.

1.

In general, size distortions increase when the first-stage coefficient
\Pi
become small, the degree of endogeneity
\rho_{\varepsilon v}
becomes high, or the IV model becomes over-identified (
d_{z}=3
).

2.

IM and CRS tests can have considerable over-rejections when the number of clusters is relatively large. For example, when
J=12
, the maximum null empirical rejection frequencies for IM are 0.185 in Table
1
and 0.209 in Table
2
, while the maximum rejection frequencies for CRS are 0.137 and 0.242, respectively.
Similarly, when
J=20
,
the maximum null rejection frequencies for IM are 0.209
in Table
8

and 0.286 in Table
9
, while those for CRS are 0.142 and 0.302, respectively, in Section
22.1
of the Online Supplement.

3.

ASY and BCH tests typically have large size distortions when the number of clusters is relatively small. For example, when
J=6
and
\Pi=0.25
, for
\rho_{\varepsilon v}=0.3,0.5,
and
0.7
, the null empirical rejection frequencies of ASY are 0.211, 0.219, and 0.228, respectively, in Table
1

and 0.254, 0.258, and 0.265, respectively, in Table
2
. BCH improves upon ASY, but still has corresponding rejection frequencies equal to 0.147, 0.154, and 0.168, respectively, in Table
1
, and 0.180, 0.185, and 0.193, respectively, in Table
2
.

4.

Among the Wald-based inference methods (IM, CRS, ASY, BCH, WRE, W-B, and W-B-S), only the three wild bootstrap tests (WRE, W-B, and W-B-S) have good size control across different settings of
J
,
\Pi
,
\rho_{\varepsilon v}
, and
d_{z}
.

5.

All the three AR-based inference methods control the size across different settings, but AR-ASY, which is based on the chi-squared critical values, can be rather conservative in the over-identified case. In particular, in Table
2
, it does not reject the null when
J=6
and has very low rejection frequencies when
J=9
in Table
9
in the Online Supplement.
28
28

28

We notice that the null rejection probabilities of the
AR_{CR,n}
-based asymptotic test decrease toward zero when
d_{z}
approaches
J
. If
d_{z}
is equal to
J
, the value of
AR_{CR,n}
will be exactly equal to
d_{z}
(or
J
), and thus has no variation (for
\bar{f}=\left(\bar{f}_{1},...,\bar{f}_{J}\right)^{\top}

and
\bar{f}_{j}=n^{-1}\sum_{i\in I_{n,j}}f_{i,j},

AR_{CR,n}=\iota_{J}^{\top}\bar{f}\left(\bar{f}^{\top}\bar{f}\right)^{-1}\bar{f}^{\top}\iota_{J}=\iota_{J}^{\top}\iota_{J}=d_{z}

as long as
\bar{f}
is invertible, where
\iota_{J}
denotes a
J
-dimensional vector of ones). By contrast, the
AR_{n}
-based bootstrap test works well even when
d_{z}
is larger than
J
.

Furthermore, for power comparisons we focus on the inference methods that control size throughout the above simulations (namely, WRE, W-B, W-B-S, AR-ASY, AR-B, and AR-B-S).
The true value of
\beta
is set equal to 1 and we vary the value of
\beta_{0}-\beta
from
-3
to
3
.

\rho_{\varepsilon v}
is set equal to
0.5
.
Figures
3
and
3
show the power curves of the bootstrap Wald tests and the AR tests, respectively, with
d_{z}=1
and
J=6
or
12
.
Figures
5
and
5
in the Online Supplement show the power curves with
d_{z}=1
and
J=9
or
20
.
In addition, Figures
7
–
9
report those for
d_{z}=3
. We highlight several observations below.

1.

In general, all the tests become more powerful when the IV strength becomes stronger and/or the number of clusters becomes larger.

2.

According to Figures
3
,
5
,

7
, and
9
, when the alternative is sufficiently distant, W-B-S (the bootstrap Wald test studentized by CRVE) is more powerful than W-B, especially when the identification is relatively weak and/or the number of clusters is small. This is in line with our theoretical result in Theorem
5.2
(ii) of Section
5
.

3.

In Figures
3
,
5
,

7
, and
9
, the power curves of WRE are between those of W-B-S and W-B in most cases and highest for certain alternatives, but they may decrease when the alternative becomes more distant, especially in the cases with relatively weak IVs and few clusters.

4.

Figures
3
,
5
,
7
, and
9
show that among the AR tests, AR-B (the wild bootstrap AR tests without CRVE studentization) has the highest power, followed by AR-B-S.
29
29

29

In the case with one IV (
d_{z}=1
), AR-B and AR-B-S are equivalent so that their power curves coincide with each other.
AR-ASY has the lowest power among the three, which is in line with the under-rejections observed in the size results.

5.

Comparing Figures
3
and
3
, we find that overall W-B-S has the best power properties among these tests. Similar observation can be made by comparing the corresponding figures of bootstrap Wald and AR tests in the Online Supplement.

DGP 2.

Table
3
reports the size for the network data.
Note the identification strength increases with
\gamma
.
Similar to DGP 1, we find that ASY and BCH does not control size when the number of clusters is small, while IM and CRS have large size distortions when the number of clusters is large so that within each cluster, the number of observations is not sufficiently large to render asymptotic normality.
Instead, the wild bootstrap-based inference methods (WRE, W-B, W-B-S, AR-B, and AR-B-S) have good size control regardless of the number of clusters.
In Section
22.2
, we further report the power results for DGP 2.
Consistent with DGP 1 and other simulation designs, we find that our W-B-S has the best power, especially against distant alternatives.

8
Empirical Application

In an influential study,
Autor
et al. 2013
analyze the effect of rising Chinese import competition on US local labor markets between 1990 and 2007, when the share of total US spending on Chinese goods increased substantially from 0.6% to 4.6%. The dataset of
Autor
et al. 2013
includes 722 commuting zones (CZs) that cover the entire mainland US.
In this section, we further analyze the region-wise effects of such import exposure by applying IV regression with the proposed wild bootstrap procedures to three Census Bureau-designated regions: South, Midwest and West, with 16, 12, and 11 states, respectively, in each region.
30
30

30

The Northeast region is not included in the study because of the relatively small number of states (
9
) and small number of CZs in each state (e.g., Connecticut and Rhode Island have only 2 CZs).

Following
Autor
et al. 2013
, we consider the linear IV model

\displaystyle y_{i,j}=X_{i,j}\beta+W_{i,j}^{\top}\gamma+\varepsilon_{i,j},

(21)

where the outcome variable
y_{i,j}
denote the decadal change in average individual log weekly wage in a given CZ and the endogenous variable
X_{i,j}
is the change in Chinese import exposure per worker in a CZ, where imports are apportioned to the CZ according to its share of national industry employment. The Bartik-type instrument
Z_{i,j}

(e.g., see
Goldsmith-Pinkham et al. 2020
)
proposed by
Autor
et al. 2013

is Chinese import growth in other high-income countries, where imports are apportioned to the CZs in the same way as that for
X_{i.j}
.
31
31

31

See Sections I.B and III.A in
Autor
et al. 2013
for a detailed definition of these variables.

As can be seen from Figure
1
, the predictive power of the IV varies considerably among states.
The exogenous variables
W_{i,j}
include the characteristic variables of CZs and decade specified in
Autor
et al. 2013
as well as state dummies.
Our regressions are based on the CZ samples in each region, and the samples are clustered at the state level, following
Autor
et al. 2013
.
Besides the results for the full sample, we also report those for female and male samples separately.
Tables
5
–
7
in the Online Supplement show the values of
\widehat{Q}_{\tilde{Z}W,j}
for each cluster in the three regions, and all of them are close to zero. (
13
) in Assumption
1
(i) is thus plausible in this application.

The main result of the IV regression for the three regions is given in Table
4
,
with the number of observations (
n
) and clusters (
J
) for each region.
As we have only one endogenous variable and one IV, the TSLS estimator is used throughout this section.
We further report the conventional 90% confidence intervals based on CRVE with the standard normal critical value (ASY) and the 90% bootstrap confidence sets (CSs) by inverting the corresponding
T_{n}
(W-B),
T_{CR,n}
(W-B-S), and
AR_{n}
(AR-B) bootstrap tests with a 10% nominal level.
32
32

32

AR-B and AR-B-S are numerically equivalent with one instrument.

The computation of the bootstrap CSs is conducted over the parameter space
[-10,10]
with a step size of 0.01, and the number of bootstrap draws is set at 2,000 for each step. We highlight the main findings below.

1.

Table
4
reports that the TSLS estimates of the average effect of Chinese imports on wages equal
-0.97
and
-1.05
for the South and West regions, respectively, while equals
-0.025
for the Midwest region.
33
33

33

That is, a $
1,000
per worker increase in a CZ’s exposure to Chinese imports is estimated to reduce average weekly earnings by
0.97
,
1.05
, and
0.025
log points, respectively, for the three regions (the corresponding TSLS estimate in
Autor
et al. 2013
for the entire mainland US is
-0.76
).
In addition, we find that the values of the effective first-stage F statistics
(
Olea and
Pflueger 2013
)
are 85.26, 8.69, and 63.45 for South, Midwest, and West, respectively, suggesting the identification is relatively weak for Midwest compared with the other two regions.
34
34

34

We used CRVE as the variance estimator of the effective first-stage F statistic. We also note that the asymptotic results and critical values for the effective F statistic in
Olea and
Pflueger 2013
are based on the consistency of the variance estimator. In the case of CRVE, this would require the number of clusters to diverge to infinity. Therefore, when the number of available clusters is small, the critical values provided by
Olea and
Pflueger 2013
might not have satisfactory performance. Still, we can learn from the values of the effective F statistics that the identification for Midwest may be relatively weak.

2.

All the three types of bootstrap CSs (W-B, W-B-S, and AR-B) are longer than the conventional asymptotic CSs (ASY) for all cases in Table
4
.
For example, the studentized Wald bootstrap CSs (W-B-S) are
22.3\%
,
26.2\%
, and
26.4\%
longer than the ASY CSs for South, Midwest, and West, respectively, in the all-sample case. Similar differences in the CS length can also be observed for female and male samples, respectively. This is in line with the simulation results in Section
7
, suggesting that conventional CSs can be too short in such cases and result in empirically relevant under-coverage.
35
35

35

We also computed CSs with BCH’s critical values, which are based on
t
distributions instead of the standard normal distribution, and find that the three types of bootstrap CSs are longer than the BCH CSs in all cases.

3.

Only the effect in the South region is significantly different from zero at the 10% level under all three bootstrap CSs, while the effects in the other two regions are not.
This is in line with the result of effective F statistics, which suggests that the identification is strongest for South.

4.

We note that the effect on West is only significant under the studentized bootstrap Wald CS (W-B-S,
[-1.22,-0.47]
). In addition, W-B-S CSs have the shortest length among the three types of bootstrap CSs in most cases in Table
4
, which is in line with our power results in Sections
5
and
7
.

5.

Comparing the results for female and male samples, we find that across all the regions, the effects are more substantial for the male samples. Furthermore, the effects for both female and male samples in the South are significantly different from zero.

9
Conclusion

In many empirical applications of IV regressions with clustered data, the IVs may be weak for some clusters and the number of clusters may be small.
In this paper, we provide valid inference methods in this context.
For the Wald tests with and without CRVE,
we extend the WRE cluster bootstrap procedure to allow for the setting of few clusters and cluster-level heterogeneity in IV strength.
For the full-vector inference, we develop wild bootstrap AR tests that control size asymptotically irrespective of IV strength, and also show that the bootstrap validity of other weak-IV-robust tests requires at least one strong IV cluster.
Furthermore, we establish the power properties of the Wald and AR tests.
Finally, recent studies by
Hansen 2022
and
MacKinnon
et al. 2023b
suggest that asymptotic or bootstrap inference based on jackknife variance estimators can be more reliable than that based on the conventional CRVE for OLS models. Therefore, it may be interesting to consider extending jackknife inference to the current setting. We leave this line of investigation for future research.

10
Pseudo-Code for the W-B-S Procedure

This section provides the pseudo-code for our recommended wild bootstrap inference procedure W-B-S. We emphasize that when constructing the bootstrapped sample
X^{*}_{i,j}(g)

and
y^{*}_{i,j}(g)

in Step 4.2 of Algorithm 1 below, we use the interacted instruments
\overline{Z}_{i,j}
defined in Step 2 of the algorithm.
However, we still use the original instruments
Z_{i,j}
to estimate the estimator and construct the test statistic
T_{CR,n}
.

input :
Data
\{y_{i,j},X_{i,j},W_{i,j},Z_{i,j}\}_{i\in[n_{j}],j\in[J]}
, the null hypothesis
\lambda_{\beta}^{\top}\beta_{0}=\lambda_{0}
.

output :
The decision of rejecting the null hypothesis.

Step 1.

Compute the studentized test statistic
T_{CR,n}
in (
6
) of the main text.

Step 2.

Construct the null-restricted and unrestricted residuals
\hat{\varepsilon}^{r}_{i,j}
and
\hat{\varepsilon}_{i,j}
, respectively, following the formulas in Section 2.3.1. Construct
\overline{Z}_{i,j}
as

\displaystyle\overline{Z}_{i,j}=\left(\widetilde{Z}^{\top}_{i,j}1\{j=1\},\cdots,\widetilde{Z}^{\top}_{i,j}1\{j=J\}\right)^{\top},

where
\widetilde{Z}_{i,j}
is the residual of regressing
Z_{i,j}
on
W_{i,j}
using the entire
sample.

Step 3.

Compute the first-stage residual
\tilde{v}_{i,j}
in (
8
) of the main text.

Step 4.

Bootstrap the test statistic.
for

b\leftarrow 1
to
B

do

Step 4.1.

Generate
g_{b}\in\{-1,1\}^{J}
so that each element of
g_{b}
is a Rademacher random variable and all elements of
g_{b}
are independent;

Step 4.2.

Use
\overline{Z}_{i,j}
,
\tilde{v}_{i,j}
,
\hat{\varepsilon}^{r}_{i,j}
, and
g_{b}
to construct
X_{i,j}^{*}(g)
and
y_{i,j}^{*}(g)
following (
9
) of the main text;

Step 4.3.

Use the bootstrapped sample
\{y_{i,j}^{*}(g_{b}),X_{i,j}^{*}(g_{b}),W_{i,j},Z_{i,j}\}_{i\in[n_{j}],j\in[J]}
to construct the bootstrapped test statistic
T^{*}_{CR,n}(g_{b})
following (
10
) of the main text.

end

Step 5.

Compute the critical value

\displaystyle\hat{c}_{CR,n}(1-\alpha)=\inf\left\{x\in\textbf{R}:\frac{1}{B}\sum_{b=1}^{B}1\{T^{*}_{CR,n}(g_{b})\leq x\}\geq 1-\alpha\right\}.

Step 6.

Reject the null hypothesis if
T_{CR,n}>\hat{c}_{CR,n}(1-\alpha)
.

Algorithm 1

Procedure for W-B-S

11
\widehat{Q}_{\tilde{Z}W,j}
in the Empirical Application

Tables
5
–
7
report the values of
\widehat{Q}_{\tilde{Z}W,j}
for each cluster in the South, Midwest, and West regions, and all of them are close to zero.
For example, in Table
5
, the column
``W_{1}"
reports the values of
\widehat{Q}_{\tilde{Z}W_{1},j}
, where
j=1,...,16
for the South region.
Therefore, (
13
) in Assumption
1
(i) is plausible in this application.

12
Equivalence Among
k
-Class Estimators

We define
\hat{\beta}_{L}
as the
k
-class estimator with
\hat{\kappa}_{L}
for
L\in\{\text{tsls},\text{liml},\text{full},\text{ba}\}
.
Their null-restricted and bootstrap counterparts are denoted as
\hat{\beta}_{L}^{r}
and
\hat{\beta}_{L,g}^{*}
, respectively, and
\hat{\gamma}_{L}
,
\hat{\gamma}_{L}^{r}
, and
\hat{\gamma}_{L,g}^{*}
are similarly defined. In the following, we show that
\hat{\beta}_{tsls}
,
\hat{\beta}_{liml}
,
\hat{\beta}_{full}
,
\hat{\beta}_{ba}
are asymptotically equivalent, and so be their null-restricted and bootstrap counterparts.

Lemma 12.1
.

Suppose Assumptions
1
,
2
, and
3
(i) hold. Then, for
L\in\{\text{liml},\text{full},\text{ba}\}
, we have

\displaystyle\hat{\beta}_{L}=\hat{\beta}_{tsls}+o_{P}(r_{n}^{-1}),\hskip 11.74988pt\hat{\beta}_{tsls}-\beta_{n}=O_{P}(r_{n}^{-1}),

\displaystyle\hat{\beta}_{L}^{r}=\hat{\beta}_{tsls}^{r}+o_{P}(r_{n}^{-1}),\hskip 11.74988pt\text{and}\hskip 11.74988pt\hat{\beta}_{tsls}^{r}-\beta_{n}=O_{P}(r_{n}^{-1}).

Proof.

First,
L\in\{\text{liml},\text{full},\text{ba}\}
, we have
\hat{\mu}_{L}=\hat{\kappa}_{L}-1
and

\displaystyle\left(\hat{\beta}^{\top}_{L},\hat{\gamma}^{\top}_{L}\right)^{\top}=

\displaystyle\left(\vec{X}^{\top}P_{\vec{Z}}\vec{X}-\hat{\mu}_{L}\vec{X}^{\top}M_{\vec{Z}}\vec{X}\right)^{-1}\left(\vec{X}^{\top}P_{\vec{Z}}Y-\hat{\mu}_{L}\vec{X}^{\top}M_{\vec{Z}}Y\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left(\Upsilon^{\top}\vec{X}\right)^{-1}\Upsilon^{\top}Y,\;\;\text{where}\;\;\Upsilon=\left[P_{\vec{Z}}X-\hat{\mu}_{L}M_{\vec{Z}}X:W\right].

Then, by applying the Frisch–Waugh–Lovell Theorem we obtain that

\displaystyle\hat{\beta}_{L}=\left(X^{\top}P_{\widetilde{Z}}X-\hat{\mu}_{L}X^{\top}M_{\vec{Z}}X\right)^{-1}\left(X^{\top}P_{\widetilde{Z}}Y-\hat{\mu}_{L}X^{\top}M_{\vec{Z}}Y\right),\;\text{where}\;\tilde{Z}=M_{W}Z.

By construction,
\hat{\beta}_{tsls}
corresponds to
\hat{\mu}_{tsls}=0
. For the LIML estimator, we have

\displaystyle\hat{\mu}_{liml}=\min_{r}r^{\top}\vec{Y}^{\top}M_{W}Z(Z^{\top}M_{W}Z)^{-1}Z^{\top}M_{W}\vec{Y}r/(r^{\top}\vec{Y}^{\top}M_{\vec{Z}}\vec{Y}r)\hskip 11.74988pt\text{and}\hskip 11.74988ptr=(1,-\beta^{\top})^{\top},

which implies that

\displaystyle n\hat{\mu}_{liml}\leq\left(\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\varepsilon^{\top}M_{W}Z\right)\left(\frac{1}{n}Z^{\top}M_{W}Z\right)^{-1}\left(\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}Z^{\top}M_{W}\varepsilon\right)/\left(\frac{1}{n}\varepsilon^{\top}M_{\vec{Z}}\varepsilon\right).

(22)

We note that

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}Z^{\top}M_{W}\varepsilon=\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\sum_{j\in[J]}\sum_{i\in I_{n,j}}\widetilde{Z}_{i,j}\varepsilon_{i,j}=O_{P}(\sqrt{n}/r_{n})\hskip 11.74988pt\text{and}

\displaystyle\left(\frac{1}{n}Z^{\top}M_{W}Z\right)^{-1}=\left(\frac{1}{n}\sum_{j\in[J]}\sum_{i\in I_{n,j}}\widetilde{Z}_{i,j}\widetilde{Z}_{i,j}^{\top}\right)^{-1}=O_{P}(1).

(23)

In addition, let
\hat{\varepsilon}_{i,j}
be the residual from the full sample projection of
\varepsilon_{i,j}
on
W_{i,j}
. Then, we have

\displaystyle\frac{1}{n}\varepsilon^{\top}M_{\vec{Z}}\varepsilon

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{n}\varepsilon^{\top}\varepsilon-\frac{1}{n}\varepsilon^{\top}\vec{Z}(\vec{Z}^{\top}\vec{Z})^{-1}\vec{Z}^{\top}\varepsilon

\displaystyle=\widehat{Q}_{\varepsilon\varepsilon}-\widehat{Q}_{\varepsilon\widetilde{Z}}\widehat{Q}_{\widetilde{Z}\widetilde{Z}}^{-1}\widehat{Q}_{\varepsilon\widetilde{Z}}^{\top}-\widehat{Q}_{\varepsilon W}\widehat{Q}_{WW}^{-1}\widehat{Q}_{\varepsilon W}^{\top}

\displaystyle=\widehat{Q}_{\varepsilon\varepsilon}-\widehat{Q}_{\varepsilon W}\widehat{Q}_{WW}^{-1}\widehat{Q}_{\varepsilon W}^{\top}+o_{P}(1)

\displaystyle=\widehat{Q}_{\tilde{\varepsilon}\tilde{\varepsilon}}+o_{P}(1)

\displaystyle=\sum_{j\in[J]}\xi_{j}\widehat{Q}_{\tilde{\varepsilon}\tilde{\varepsilon},j}+o_{P}(1)

\displaystyle\geq\sum_{j\in[J]}\xi_{j}\widehat{Q}_{\dot{\varepsilon}\dot{\varepsilon},j}+o_{P}(1)

\displaystyle=\widehat{Q}_{\dot{\varepsilon}\dot{\varepsilon}}+o_{P}(1)\geq c\penalty\ w.p.a.1,

(24)

where
c
is a positive constant, the first inequality is by the definition that
\dot{\varepsilon}_{i,j}
is the residual from the cluster-level projection of
\varepsilon_{i,j}
on
W_{i,j}
, and the second inequality is by Assumption
2
(iii). Combining (
22
)–(
24
), we have
\hat{\mu}_{liml}=O_{P}(r_{n}^{-2})
. In addition, we have
\frac{1}{n}X^{\top}M_{\vec{Z}}X=O_{P}(1)
,
\frac{1}{n}X^{\top}M_{\vec{Z}}Y=O_{P}(1)
, and

\displaystyle\left(\frac{1}{n}X^{\top}P_{\widetilde{Z}}X\right)^{-1}=\left(\widehat{Q}_{X\widetilde{Z}}\widehat{Q}_{\widetilde{Z}\widetilde{Z}}^{-1}\widehat{Q}_{\widetilde{Z}X}\right)^{-1}=O_{P}(1),

(25)

where the last equality holds by Assumptions
2
(ii) and
3
(i). This means

\displaystyle\hat{\beta}_{liml}=\hat{\beta}_{tsls}+o_{P}(r_{n}^{-1}).

In addition, we note
\hat{\mu}_{full}=\hat{\mu}_{liml}-\frac{C}{n-d_{z}-d_{w}}=O_{P}(r_{n}^{-2})
and
\hat{\mu}_{ba}=O(n^{-1})
, respectively. Therefore, we have the same results for
(\hat{\beta}_{full},\hat{\beta}_{ba})
.

For the second statement in the lemma, we have

\displaystyle(\hat{\beta}_{tsls}-\beta_{n})

\displaystyle=\left(X^{\top}P_{\widetilde{Z}}X\right)^{-1}X^{\top}P_{\widetilde{Z}}\varepsilon=\left(\widehat{Q}_{X\widetilde{Z}}\widehat{Q}_{\widetilde{Z}\widetilde{Z}}^{-1}\widehat{Q}_{\widetilde{Z}X}\right)^{-1}\widehat{Q}_{X\widetilde{Z}}\widehat{Q}_{\widetilde{Z}\widetilde{Z}}^{-1}\widehat{Q}_{\widetilde{Z}\varepsilon}=O_{P}(r_{n}^{-1}),

where the last equality holds because
\widehat{Q}_{\widetilde{Z}\varepsilon}=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{j\in[J]}\sum_{i\in I_{n,j}}\widetilde{Z}_{i,j}\varepsilon_{i,j}=O_{P}(r_{n}^{-1})
.

Next, we turn to the third statement in the lemma. We note that, for
L\in\{\text{tsls},\text{liml},\text{full},\text{ba}\}
and

\lambda_{\beta}^{\top}\hat{\beta}^{r}_{L}=\lambda_{0}
,

\displaystyle\hat{\beta}^{r}_{L}=\hat{\beta}_{L}-\left(X^{\top}(P_{\widetilde{Z}}-\hat{\mu}_{L}M_{\vec{Z}})X\right)^{-1}\lambda_{\beta}\left(\lambda_{\beta}^{\top}(X^{\top}(P_{\widetilde{Z}}-\hat{\mu}_{L}M_{\vec{Z}})X)^{-1}\lambda_{\beta}\right)^{-1}(\lambda_{\beta}^{\top}\hat{\beta}_{L}-\lambda_{0})=O_{P}(1).

As
\hat{\mu}_{L}=O_{P}(r_{n}^{-2})
and
\hat{\beta}_{L}=\hat{\beta}_{tsls}+o_{P}(r_{n}^{-1})
for
L\in\{\text{liml},\text{full},\text{ba}\}
, we have

\displaystyle\hat{\beta}^{r}_{L}=\hat{\beta}_{tsls}-\left(X^{\top}P_{\widetilde{Z}}X\right)^{-1}\lambda_{\beta}\left(\lambda_{\beta}^{\top}(X^{\top}P_{\widetilde{Z}}X)^{-1}\lambda_{\beta}\right)^{-1}(\lambda_{\beta}^{\top}\hat{\beta}_{tsls}-\lambda_{0})+o_{P}(r_{n}^{-1})=\hat{\beta}^{r}_{tsls}+o_{P}(r_{n}^{-1}).

For the last statement in the lemma, we note that

\displaystyle\hat{\beta}^{r}_{tsls}-\beta_{n}

\displaystyle=(\hat{\beta}_{tsls}-\beta_{n})-\left(X^{\top}P_{\widetilde{Z}}X\right)^{-1}\lambda_{\beta}\left(\lambda_{\beta}^{\top}(X^{\top}P_{\widetilde{Z}}X)^{-1}\lambda_{\beta}\right)^{-1}\lambda_{\beta}^{\top}(\hat{\beta}_{tsls}-\beta_{n})

\displaystyle-\left(X^{\top}P_{\widetilde{Z}}X\right)^{-1}\lambda_{\beta}\left(\lambda_{\beta}^{\top}(X^{\top}P_{\widetilde{Z}}X)^{-1}\lambda_{\beta}\right)^{-1}(\lambda_{\beta}^{\top}\beta_{n}-\lambda_{0})=O_{P}(r_{n}^{-1}),

where the last equality holds because
\lambda_{\beta}^{\top}\beta_{n}-\lambda_{0}=\mu r_{n}^{-1}
by construction and
\hat{\beta}_{tsls}-\beta_{n}=O_{P}(r_{n}^{-1})
.
\blacksquare

Lemma 12.2
.

Suppose Assumptions
1
,
2
, and
3
(i) hold and
\widehat{Q}_{\widetilde{Z}W,j}(\hat{\gamma}_{L}^{r}-\gamma)=o_{P}(r_{n}^{-1})
. Then, for
L\in\{\text{liml},\text{full},\text{ba}\}
and
g\in\textbf{G}
, we have

\displaystyle\hat{\beta}_{L,g}^{*}=\hat{\beta}_{tsls,g}^{*}+o_{P}(r_{n}^{-1})\hskip 11.74988pt\text{and}\hskip 11.74988pt\hat{\beta}_{tsls,g}^{*}-\beta_{n}=O_{P}(r_{n}^{-1}).

(26)

Proof.
By the same argument in the proof of Lemma
12.1
, for
L\in\{\text{tsls},\text{liml},\text{full},\text{ba}\}
and
g\in\textbf{G}
, we have

\displaystyle\hat{\beta}^{*}_{L,g}=\left(X^{*\top}(g)P_{\widetilde{Z}}X^{*}(g)-\hat{\mu}^{*}_{L,g}X^{*\top}(g)M_{\vec{Z}}X^{*}(g)\right)^{-1}\left(X^{*\top}(g)P_{\widetilde{Z}}Y^{*}(g)-\hat{\mu}^{*}_{L,g}X^{*\top}(g)M_{\vec{Z}}Y^{*}(g)\right).

(27)

such that
\hat{\mu}_{tsls,g}^{*}=0
,
\hat{\mu}_{full,g}^{*}=\hat{\mu}_{liml,g}^{*}-\frac{C}{n-d_{z}-d_{x}}
,
\hat{\mu}_{ba,g}^{*}=\hat{\mu}_{ba}
, and

\hat{\mu}^{*}_{liml,g}=\min_{r}r^{\top}\vec{Y}^{*\top}(g)M_{W}Z(Z^{\top}M_{W}Z)^{-1}Z^{\top}M_{W}\vec{Y}^{*}(g)r/(r^{\top}\vec{Y}^{*\top}(g)M_{\vec{Z}}\vec{Y}^{*}(g)r),

where
\vec{Y}^{*}(g)=[Y^{*}(g):X^{*}(g)]
,
Y^{*}(g)
is an
n\times 1
vector formed by
Y_{i,j}^{*}(g)
, and
r=(1,-\beta^{\top})^{\top}
.

Following the same argument previously, we have

\displaystyle n\hat{\mu}_{liml,g}^{*}\leq\left(\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\varepsilon_{g}^{*r\top}M_{W}Z\right)\left(\frac{1}{n}Z^{\top}M_{W}Z\right)^{-1}\left(\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}Z^{\top}M_{W}\varepsilon_{g}^{*r}\right)/\left(\frac{1}{n}\varepsilon_{g}^{*r\top}M_{\vec{Z}}\varepsilon_{g}^{*r}\right),

(28)

where
\varepsilon_{g}^{*r}
is an
n\times 1
vector formed by
g_{j}\hat{\varepsilon}_{i,j}^{r}
. We first note that

\displaystyle\frac{1}{n}\varepsilon_{g}^{*r\top}M_{W}Z

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{j\in[J]}\sum_{i\in I_{n,j}}g_{j}\widetilde{Z}_{i,j}\hat{\varepsilon}^{r}_{i,j}

\displaystyle=\sum_{j\in[J]}g_{j}\left[\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i\in I_{n,j}}\widetilde{Z}_{i,j}\varepsilon_{i,j}+\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i\in I_{n,j}}\widetilde{Z}_{i,j}X_{i,j}^{\top}(\beta_{n}-\hat{\beta}^{r}_{L})+\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i\in I_{n,j}}\widetilde{Z}_{i,j}W_{i,j}^{\top}(\gamma-\hat{\gamma}^{r}_{L})\right]

\displaystyle=O_{P}(r_{n}^{-1}),

(29)

where the last line is by Assumptions
1
(ii), Lemma
12.1
, and the assumption that
\widehat{Q}_{\widetilde{Z}W,j}(\hat{\gamma}_{L}^{r}-\gamma)=o_{P}(r_{n}^{-1})
. Second, we have

\displaystyle\frac{1}{n}\varepsilon_{g}^{*r\top}M_{\vec{Z}}\varepsilon_{g}^{*r}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{n}\varepsilon_{g}^{*r\top}\varepsilon_{g}^{*r}-\frac{1}{n}\varepsilon_{g}^{*r\top}\vec{Z}(\vec{Z}^{\top}\vec{Z})^{-1}\vec{Z}^{\top}\varepsilon_{g}^{*r}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{j\in[J]}\sum_{i\in I_{n,j}}(g_{j}\hat{\varepsilon}_{i,j}^{r})^{2}-\left[\frac{1}{n}\sum_{j\in[J]}\sum_{i\in I_{n,j}}(g_{j}\hat{\varepsilon}_{i,j}^{r}\widetilde{Z}_{i,j}^{\top})\right]\widehat{Q}_{\widetilde{Z}\widetilde{Z}}^{-1}\left[\frac{1}{n}\sum_{j\in[J]}\sum_{i\in I_{n,j}}(g_{j}\hat{\varepsilon}_{i,j}^{r}\widetilde{Z}_{i,j})\right]

\displaystyle-\left[\frac{1}{n}\sum_{j\in[J]}\sum_{i\in I_{n,j}}(g_{j}\hat{\varepsilon}_{i,j}^{r}W_{i,j}^{\top})\right]\widehat{Q}_{WW}^{-1}\left[\frac{1}{n}\sum_{j\in[J]}\sum_{i\in I_{n,j}}(g_{j}\hat{\varepsilon}_{i,j}^{r}W_{i,j})\right]

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{j\in[J]}\sum_{i\in I_{n,j}}(\hat{\varepsilon}_{i,j}^{r})^{2}-\left[\frac{1}{n}\sum_{j\in[J]}\sum_{i\in I_{n,j}}(g_{j}\hat{\varepsilon}_{i,j}^{r}W_{i,j}^{\top})\right]\widehat{Q}_{WW}^{-1}\left[\frac{1}{n}\sum_{j\in[J]}\sum_{i\in I_{n,j}}(g_{j}\hat{\varepsilon}_{i,j}^{r}W_{i,j})\right]+o_{P}(1),

(30)

where the last equality is by
\frac{1}{n_{j}}\sum_{i\in I_{n,j}}\hat{\varepsilon}^{r}_{i,j}\widetilde{Z}_{i,j}=O_{P}(r_{n}^{-1})
, as in (
29
). We further note that

\displaystyle\hat{\varepsilon}^{r}_{i,j}=\varepsilon_{i,j}-X_{i,j}^{\top}(\hat{\beta}_{L}^{r}-\beta_{n})-W_{i,j}^{\top}(\hat{\gamma}_{L}^{r}-\gamma),

where
\hat{\beta}_{L}^{r}-\beta_{n}=O_{P}(r_{n}^{-1})
and

\displaystyle\hat{\gamma}_{L}^{r}-\gamma=\widehat{Q}_{WW}^{-1}\left[\frac{1}{n}\sum_{j\in[J]}\sum_{i\in I_{n,j}}W_{i,j}\left(Y_{i,j}-X_{i,j}^{\top}\hat{\beta}_{L}^{r}\right)\right]-\gamma=\widehat{Q}_{WW}^{-1}\widehat{Q}_{W\varepsilon}+O_{P}(r_{n}^{-1}).

Therefore, we have

\displaystyle\frac{1}{n}\sum_{j\in[J]}\sum_{i\in I_{n,j}}(\hat{\varepsilon}_{i,j}^{r})^{2}=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{j\in[J]}\sum_{i\in I_{n,j}}\mathscr{E}_{i,j}^{2}+o_{P}(1),

where
\mathscr{E}_{i,j}=\varepsilon_{i,j}-W_{i,j}^{\top}\widehat{Q}_{WW}^{-1}\widehat{Q}_{W\varepsilon}.
Similarly, we can show that

\displaystyle\left[\frac{1}{n}\sum_{j\in[J]}\sum_{i\in I_{n,j}}(g_{j}\hat{\varepsilon}_{i,j}^{r}W_{i,j}^{\top})\right]\widehat{Q}_{WW}^{-1}\left[\frac{1}{n}\sum_{j\in[J]}\sum_{i\in I_{n,j}}(g_{j}\hat{\varepsilon}_{i,j}^{r}W_{i,j})\right]

\displaystyle=\left[\frac{1}{n}\sum_{j\in[J]}\sum_{i\in I_{n,j}}(g_{j}\mathscr{E}_{i,j}W_{i,j}^{\top})\right]\widehat{Q}_{WW}^{-1}\left[\frac{1}{n}\sum_{j\in[J]}\sum_{i\in I_{n,j}}(g_{j}\mathscr{E}_{i,j}W_{i,j})\right]+o_{P}(1),

which, combined with (
30
), implies

\displaystyle\frac{1}{n}\varepsilon_{g}^{*r\top}M_{\vec{Z}}\varepsilon_{g}^{*r}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{j\in[J]}\sum_{i\in I_{n,j}}\mathscr{E}_{i,j}^{2}-\left[\frac{1}{n}\sum_{j\in[J]}\sum_{i\in I_{n,j}}(g_{j}\mathscr{E}_{i,j}W_{i,j}^{\top})\right]\widehat{Q}_{WW}^{-1}\left[\frac{1}{n}\sum_{j\in[J]}\sum_{i\in I_{n,j}}(g_{j}\mathscr{E}_{i,j}W_{i,j})\right]+o_{P}(1)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{j\in[J]}\sum_{i\in I_{n,j}}(\mathscr{E}_{i,j}-g_{j}W_{i,j}^{\top}\hat{\theta}_{g})^{2}+o_{P}(1),

where
\hat{\theta}_{g}=\widehat{Q}_{WW}^{-1}\left[\frac{1}{n}\sum_{j\in[J]}\sum_{i\in I_{n,j}}(g_{j}\mathscr{E}_{i,j}W_{i,j})\right]
and the second equality holds because

\displaystyle\widehat{Q}_{WW}=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{j\in[J]}\sum_{i\in I_{n,j}}W_{i,j}W_{i,j}^{\top}=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{j\in[J]}\sum_{i\in I_{n,j}}(g_{j}W_{i,j})(g_{j}W_{i,j})^{\top}.

Recall that
\dot{\varepsilon}_{i,j}
is the residual from the cluster-level projection of
\varepsilon_{i,j}
on
W_{i,j}
, which means there exists a vector
\hat{\theta}_{j}
such that
\varepsilon_{i,j}=\dot{\varepsilon}_{i,j}+W_{i,j}^{\top}\hat{\theta}_{j}
and
\sum_{i\in I_{n,j}}\dot{\varepsilon}_{i,j}W_{i,j}=0
. Then, we have

\displaystyle\frac{1}{n}\sum_{j\in[J]}\sum_{i\in I_{n,j}}(\mathscr{E}_{i,j}-g_{j}W_{i,j}^{\top}\hat{\theta}_{g})^{2}=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{j\in[J]}\left[\sum_{i\in I_{n,j}}\dot{\varepsilon}_{i,j}^{2}+\sum_{i\in I_{n,j}}(W_{i,j}^{\top}\hat{\theta}_{j}-g_{j}W_{i,j}^{\top}\hat{\theta}_{g})^{2}\right]\geq\frac{1}{n}\sum_{j\in[J]}\sum_{i\in I_{n,j}}\dot{\varepsilon}_{i,j}^{2}\geq c\penalty\ w.p.a.1,

where the last inequality is by Assumption
2
(iii). This implies

\displaystyle\frac{1}{n}\varepsilon_{g}^{*r\top}M_{\vec{Z}}\varepsilon_{g}^{*r}

\displaystyle\geq c-o_{P}(1).

(31)

Combining (
28
), (
29
), and (
31
), we obtain that
\hat{\mu}_{liml,g}^{*}=O_{P}(r_{n}^{-2})
, and thus,
\hat{\mu}^{*}_{full,g}=O_{P}(r_{n}^{-2})
. It is also obvious that
\hat{\mu}^{*}_{ba,g}=O_{P}(n^{-1})
. Given that
\hat{\mu}^{*}_{L,g}=O_{P}(r_{n}^{-2}+n^{-1})
,
to establish
\hat{\beta}_{L,g}^{*}=\hat{\beta}_{tsls,g}^{*}+o_{P}(r_{n}^{-1})
,
it suffices to show
\frac{1}{n}X^{*\top}(g)M_{\vec{Z}}X^{*}(g)=O_{P}(1)
,
\frac{1}{n}X^{*\top}(g)M_{\vec{Z}}Y^{*}(g)=O_{P}(1)
, and
(\frac{1}{n}X^{*\top}(g)P_{\widetilde{Z}}X^{*}(g))^{-1}=O_{P}(1)
.

For
\frac{1}{n}X^{*\top}(g)M_{\vec{Z}}Y^{*}(g)=\frac{1}{n}X^{*\top}(g)Y^{*}(g)-\frac{1}{n}X^{*\top}(g)P_{\vec{Z}}Y^{*}(g)
, we first show

\displaystyle\frac{1}{n}X^{*\top}(g)Y^{*}(g)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{j\in[J]}\sum_{i\in I_{n,j}}X^{*}_{i,j}(g)\left(X_{i,j}^{*\top}(g)\hat{\beta}^{r}_{L}+W_{i,j}\hat{\gamma}^{r}_{L}+g_{j}\hat{\varepsilon}^{r}_{i,j}\right)

\displaystyle=\widehat{Q}^{*}_{XX}(g)\hat{\beta}^{r}_{L}+\widehat{Q}^{*}_{XW}(g)\hat{\gamma}^{r}_{L}+\widehat{Q}^{*}_{X\varepsilon}(g)=O_{P}(1),

(32)

where
\widehat{Q}^{*}_{XX}(g)=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{j\in[J]}\sum_{i\in I_{n,j}}X^{*}_{i,j}(g)X^{*\top}_{i,j}(g)
,

\widehat{Q}^{*}_{XW}(g)=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{j\in[J]}\sum_{i\in I_{n,j}}X^{*}_{i,j}(g)W^{\top}_{i,j}
, and

\widehat{Q}^{*}_{X\varepsilon}(g)=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{j\in[J]}\sum_{i\in I_{n,j}}g_{j}X^{*}_{i,j}(g)\hat{\varepsilon}^{r}_{i,j}
.
Notice that

\displaystyle\widehat{Q}^{*}_{XX}(g)=\sum_{j\in[J]}\frac{n_{j}}{n}\frac{1}{n_{j}}\sum_{i\in I_{n,j}}\left(X_{i,j}+(g_{j}-1)\tilde{v}_{i,j}\right)\left(X_{i,j}+(g_{j}-1)\tilde{v}_{i,j}\right)^{\top}=O_{P}(1)

(33)

because
\widehat{Q}_{XX,j}=O_{P}(1)
,
\widehat{Q}_{X\tilde{v},j}=O_{P}(1)
,
and
\widehat{Q}_{\tilde{v}\tilde{v},j}=O_{P}(1)
. To see these three relations, we note that

\displaystyle\widehat{Q}_{X\tilde{v},j}=\widehat{Q}_{XX,j}-\widehat{Q}_{X\overline{Z},j}\widetilde{\Pi}_{\overline{Z}}-\widehat{Q}_{XW,j}\widetilde{\Pi}_{w}=O_{P}(1),

by
\widehat{Q}_{XX,j}=O_{P}(1)
,
\widehat{Q}_{X\overline{Z},j}=O_{P}(1)
,

\widehat{Q}_{XW,j}=O_{P}(1)
,
\tilde{\Pi}_{\overline{Z}}=O_{P}(1)
, and
\widetilde{\Pi}_{w}=O_{P}(1)
,
where
\widehat{Q}_{X\overline{Z},j}=\frac{1}{n_{j}}\sum_{i\in I_{n,j}}X_{i,j}\overline{Z}_{i,j}^{\top}
, and

\widehat{Q}_{XW,j}=\frac{1}{n_{j}}\sum_{i\in I_{n,j}}X_{i,j}W_{i,j}^{\top}
.
Similar arguments hold for
\widehat{Q}_{\tilde{v}\tilde{v},j}
.
We can also show that

\displaystyle\widehat{Q}^{*}_{XW}(g)=\sum_{j\in[J]}\frac{n_{j}}{n}\frac{1}{n_{j}}\sum_{i\in I_{n,j}}\left(X_{i,j}+(g_{j}-1)\tilde{v}_{i,j}\right)W_{i,j}^{\top}=O_{P}(1),

(34)

by
\widehat{Q}_{XW,j}=O_{P}(1)
and
\widehat{Q}_{\tilde{v}W,j}=O_{P}(1)
,
where
\widehat{Q}_{XW,j}=\frac{1}{n_{j}}\sum_{i\in I_{n,j}}X_{i,j}W_{i,j}^{\top}

and
\widehat{Q}_{\tilde{v}W,j}=\frac{1}{n_{j}}\sum_{i\in I_{n,j}}\tilde{v}_{i,j}W_{i,j}^{\top}
.
In addition, we have

\displaystyle\widehat{Q}^{*}_{X\varepsilon}(g)=\sum_{j\in[J]}\frac{n_{j}}{n}\frac{1}{n_{j}}\sum_{i\in I_{n,j}}g_{j}\left(X_{i,j}+(g_{j}-1)\tilde{v}_{i,j}\right)\hat{\varepsilon}^{r}_{i,j}=O_{P}(1),

(35)

by
\widehat{Q}_{X\hat{\varepsilon},j}=O_{P}(1)
and
\widehat{Q}_{\tilde{v}\hat{\varepsilon},j}=O_{P}(1)
under similar arguments as those in (
29
),
where

\displaystyle\widehat{Q}_{X\hat{\varepsilon},j}=\frac{1}{n_{j}}\sum_{i\in I_{n,j}}X_{i,j}\hat{\varepsilon}_{i,j}^{r}\hskip 11.74988pt\text{and}\hskip 11.74988pt\widehat{Q}_{\tilde{v}\hat{\varepsilon},j}=\frac{1}{n_{j}}\sum_{i\in I_{n,j}}\tilde{v}_{i,j}\hat{\varepsilon}_{i,j}^{r}.

Combining (
33
), (
34
), (
35
),
\hat{\beta}^{r}_{L}=O_{P}(1)
,
and
\hat{\gamma}^{r}_{L}=\widehat{Q}_{WW}^{-1}\widehat{Q}_{w\varepsilon}+o_{P}(1)=O_{P}(1)
, we obtain (
12
). Next, by the fact that
\frac{1}{n}\sum_{j\in[J]}\sum_{i\in I_{n,j}}\widetilde{Z}_{i,j}W_{i,j}^{\top}=0
, we have

\displaystyle\frac{1}{n}X^{*\top}(g)P_{\vec{Z}}Y^{*}(g)=\begin{pmatrix}\widehat{Q}_{\widetilde{Z}X}^{*\top}(g)&\widehat{Q}_{XW}^{*}(g)\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}\widehat{Q}_{\widetilde{Z}\widetilde{Z}}^{-1}&0\\
0&\widehat{Q}_{WW}^{-1}\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}\widehat{Q}_{\widetilde{Z}Y}^{*}(g)\\
\widehat{Q}_{WY}^{*}(g)\end{pmatrix},

where

\displaystyle\widehat{Q}_{\widetilde{Z}X}^{*}(g)=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{j\in[J]}\sum_{i\in I_{n,j}}\widetilde{Z}_{i,j}X_{i,j}^{*\top}(g),\hskip 11.74988pt\widehat{Q}_{\widetilde{Z}Y}^{*}(g)=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{j\in[J]}\sum_{i\in I_{n,j}}\widetilde{Z}_{i,j}Y_{i,j}^{*}(g),

\displaystyle\text{and}\hskip 11.74988pt\widehat{Q}_{WY}^{*}(g)=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{j\in[J]}\sum_{i\in I_{n,j}}W_{i,j}Y_{i,j}^{*}(g).

Following the same lines of reasoning, we can show that, for all
g\in\textbf{G}
,
\widehat{Q}_{\widetilde{Z}X}^{*}(g)=O_{P}(1)
,
\widehat{Q}_{\widetilde{Z}Y}^{*}(g)=O_{P}(1)
, and
\widehat{Q}_{WY}^{*}(g)=O_{P}(1)
. In addition, by Assumptions
1
(iv) and
2
(iii),
\widehat{Q}_{\widetilde{Z}\widetilde{Z}}^{-1}=O_{P}(1)
and
\widehat{Q}_{WW}^{-1}=O_{P}(1)
, which further implies that
\frac{1}{n}X^{*\top}(g)M_{\vec{Z}}Y^{*}(g)=O_{P}(1)
.

By a similar argument, we can show
\frac{1}{n}X^{*\top}(g)M_{\vec{Z}}X^{*}(g)=O_{P}(1)
. Last, we have

\displaystyle\frac{1}{n}X^{*\top}(g)P_{\widetilde{Z}}X^{*}(g)=\widehat{Q}_{\widetilde{Z}X}^{*\top}(g)\widehat{Q}_{\widetilde{Z}\widetilde{Z}}^{-1}\widehat{Q}_{\widetilde{Z}X}^{*}(g)=

\displaystyle\widehat{Q}_{\widetilde{Z}X}^{\top}\widehat{Q}_{\widetilde{Z}\widetilde{Z}}^{-1}\widehat{Q}_{\widetilde{Z}X}+o_{P}(1)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle p}}{{\longrightarrow}}Q_{\widetilde{Z}X}^{\top}Q_{\widetilde{Z}\widetilde{Z}}^{-1}Q_{\widetilde{Z}X},

where we use the fact that
\widehat{Q}_{\widetilde{Z}X}^{*}(g)=\widehat{Q}_{\widetilde{Z}X}+o_{P}(1)
, which is established in Step 1 in the proof of Theorem
4.1
. In addition,
Q_{\widetilde{Z}X}^{\top}Q_{\widetilde{Z}\widetilde{Z}}^{-1}Q_{\widetilde{Z}X}
is invertible by Assumptions
2
(ii) and
3
(i). This implies
(\frac{1}{n}X^{*\top}(g)P_{\widetilde{Z}}X^{*}(g))^{-1}=O_{P}(1)
, which further implies

\hat{\beta}_{L,g}^{*}=\hat{\beta}_{tsls,g}^{*}+o_{P}(r_{n}^{-1})
.

For the second result, we note that

\displaystyle\hat{\beta}_{tsls,g}^{*}-\beta_{n}

\displaystyle=\left[\widehat{Q}_{\widetilde{Z}X}^{*\top}(g)\widehat{Q}_{\widetilde{Z}\widetilde{Z}}^{-1}\widehat{Q}_{\widetilde{Z}X}^{*}(g)\right]^{-1}\widehat{Q}_{\widetilde{Z}X}^{*\top}(g)\widehat{Q}_{\widetilde{Z}\widetilde{Z}}^{-1}\left[\frac{1}{n}\sum_{j\in[J]}\sum_{i\in I_{n,j}}\widetilde{Z}_{i,j}y_{i,j}^{*}(g)\right]-\beta_{n}

\displaystyle=\hat{\beta}_{tsls}^{r}-\beta_{n}+\left[\widehat{Q}_{\widetilde{Z}X}^{*\top}(g)\widehat{Q}_{\widetilde{Z}\widetilde{Z}}^{-1}\widehat{Q}_{\widetilde{Z}X}^{*}(g)\right]^{-1}\widehat{Q}_{\widetilde{Z}X}^{*\top}(g)\widehat{Q}_{\widetilde{Z}\widetilde{Z}}^{-1}\left[\frac{1}{n}\sum_{j\in[J]}\sum_{i\in I_{n,j}}g_{j}\widetilde{Z}_{i,j}\hat{\varepsilon}_{i,j}^{r}\right],

where the second equality holds because
\sum_{j\in[J]}\sum_{i\in I_{n,j}}g_{j}\widetilde{Z}_{i,j}W_{i,j}^{\top}=0.
In addition, note that

\displaystyle\frac{1}{n_{j}}\sum_{i\in I_{n,j}}\widetilde{Z}_{i,j}\hat{\varepsilon}_{i,j}^{r}=\frac{1}{n_{j}}\sum_{i\in I_{n,j}}\widetilde{Z}_{i,j}\varepsilon_{i,j}-\widehat{Q}_{\widetilde{Z}X,j}(\hat{\beta}_{tsls}^{r}-\beta_{n})-\widehat{Q}_{\widetilde{Z}W,j}(\hat{\gamma}_{tsls}^{r}-\gamma)=O_{P}(r_{n}^{-1}).

Combining this with the fact that
\hat{\beta}_{tsls}^{r}-\beta_{n}=O_{P}(r_{n}^{-1})
as shown in Lemma
12.1
, we have
\hat{\beta}_{tsls,g}^{*}-\beta_{n}=O_{P}(r_{n}^{-1})
.
\blacksquare

Lemma 12.3
.

Suppose Assumptions
1
and
2
hold. Then, we have, for
j\in[J]
,

\widehat{Q}_{\widetilde{Z}W,j}(\bar{\gamma}^{r}-\gamma)=o_{P}(r_{n}^{-1}).

If, in addition, Assumption
3
(i) holds, then we have, For
j\in[J]
,
L\in\{\text{tsls},\text{liml},\text{full},\text{ba}\}
, and
g\in\textbf{G}
,

\displaystyle\widehat{Q}_{\widetilde{Z}W,j}(\hat{\gamma}_{L}-\gamma)=o_{P}(r_{n}^{-1}),\hskip 11.74988pt\widehat{Q}_{\widetilde{Z}W,j}(\hat{\gamma}^{r}_{L}-\gamma)=o_{P}(r_{n}^{-1}),\hskip 11.74988pt\text{and}\hskip 11.74988pt\widehat{Q}_{\widetilde{Z}W,j}(\hat{\gamma}^{*}_{L,g}-\gamma)=o_{P}(r_{n}^{-1}).

Proof.

Note that we assume
\widehat{Q}_{\widetilde{Z}W,j}=o_{P}(1)
and
\frac{1}{n}\sum_{j\in[J]}\sum_{i\in I_{n,j}}W_{i,j}\varepsilon_{i,j}=O_{P}(r_{n}^{-1})
. The first statement holds because

\displaystyle\bar{\gamma}^{r}-\gamma

\displaystyle=\widehat{Q}_{WW}^{-1}\left[\frac{1}{n}\sum_{j\in[J]}\sum_{i\in I_{n,j}}W_{i,j}(Y_{i,j}-X_{i,j}^{\top}\beta_{0})\right]-\gamma

\displaystyle=\widehat{Q}_{WW}^{-1}\left[\frac{1}{n}\sum_{j\in[J]}\sum_{i\in I_{n,j}}W_{i,j}\varepsilon_{i,j}\right]-\widehat{Q}_{WW}^{-1}\widehat{Q}_{WX}(\beta_{n}-\beta_{0})=O_{P}(r_{n}^{-1}),

(36)

Next, if Assumption
3
(i) also holds, then

\displaystyle\hat{\gamma}_{L}-\gamma

\displaystyle=\widehat{Q}_{WW}^{-1}\left[\frac{1}{n}\sum_{j\in[J]}\sum_{i\in I_{n,j}}W_{i,j}(Y_{i,j}-X_{i,j}^{\top}\hat{\beta}_{L})\right]-\gamma

\displaystyle=\widehat{Q}_{WW}^{-1}\left[\frac{1}{n}\sum_{j\in[J]}\sum_{i\in I_{n,j}}W_{i,j}\varepsilon_{i,j}\right]-\widehat{Q}_{WW}^{-1}\widehat{Q}_{WX}(\hat{\beta}_{L}-\beta_{n})=O_{P}(r_{n}^{-1}),

(37)

where the last equality holds by Lemma
12.1
. This implies
\widehat{Q}_{\widetilde{Z}W,j}(\hat{\gamma}_{L}-\gamma)=o_{P}(r_{n}^{-1})
. In the same manner, we can show
\widehat{Q}_{\widetilde{Z}W,j}(\hat{\gamma}_{L}^{r}-\gamma)=o_{P}(r_{n}^{-1})
. Last, given Assumptions
1
,
2
,
3
(i), and the fact that
\widehat{Q}_{\widetilde{Z}W,j}(\hat{\gamma}_{L}^{r}-\gamma)=o_{P}(r_{n}^{-1})
, Lemma
12.2
shows
\hat{\beta}_{L,g}^{*}-\beta_{n}=(\hat{\beta}_{L,g}^{*}-\hat{\beta}_{tsls,g}^{*})+(\hat{\beta}_{tsls,g}^{*}-\beta_{n})=O_{P}(r_{n}^{-1})
. Then, following the same argument in (
37
), we can show
\hat{\gamma}_{L,g}^{*}-\gamma=O_{P}(r_{n}^{-1})
, which leads to the desired result.

\blacksquare

13
Proof of Theorem
4.1

When
d_{x}=1
, we define
a_{j}=Q^{-1}Q_{\widetilde{Z}X}^{\top}Q_{\widetilde{Z}\widetilde{Z}}^{-1}Q_{\widetilde{Z}X,j}
. For
d_{x}>2
,
a_{j}
is defined in Assumption
3
. Let
\mathbb{S}\equiv\textbf{R}^{d_{z}\times d_{x}}\times\textbf{R}^{d_{z}\times d_{z}}\times\otimes_{j\in[J]}\textbf{R}^{d_{z}}\times\textbf{R}^{d_{r}\times d_{r}}
and write an element
s\in\mathbb{S}

by
s=\left(s_{1},s_{2},\{s_{3,j}:j\in[J]\},s_{4}\right)
where
s_{3,j}\in\textbf{R}^{d_{z}}
for any
j\in[J]
.
Define the function
T
:
\mathbb{S}\rightarrow\textbf{R}
to be given by

\displaystyle T(s)=\bigg\|\lambda_{\beta}^{\top}\left(s_{1}^{\top}s_{2}^{-1}s_{1}\right)^{-1}s_{1}^{\top}s_{2}^{-1}\left(\sum_{j\in[J]}s_{3,j}\right)\bigg\|_{s_{4}}

(38)

for any
s\in\mathbb{S}
such that
s_{2}
and
s_{1}^{\top}s_{2}^{-1}s_{1}
are invertible and let
T(s)=0
otherwise.
We also identify any
(g_{1},...,g_{q})=g\in\textbf{G}=\{-1,1\}^{J}
with an action on
s\in\mathbb{S}

given by
gs=\left(s_{1},s_{2},\{g_{j}s_{3,j}:j\in[J]\},s_{4}\right)
.
For any
s\in\mathbb{S}
and
\textbf{G'}\subseteq\textbf{G}
, denote the ordered values of

\{T(gs):g\in\textbf{G'}\}
by

T^{(1)}(s|\textbf{G'})\leq\ldots\leq T^{(|\textbf{G'}|)}(s|\textbf{G'})
.
In addition, for any
\textbf{G'}\subseteq\textbf{G}
, denote the ordered values of

\{T^{*}_{n}(g):g\in\textbf{G'}\}
by

T^{*(1)}_{n}(\textbf{G'})\leq\ldots\leq T_{n}^{*(|\textbf{G'}|)}(\textbf{G'})
.

Given this notation we can define the statistics
S_{n},\widehat{S}_{n}\in\mathbb{S}
as

\displaystyle S_{n}

\displaystyle=\left(\widehat{Q}_{\widetilde{Z}X},\widehat{Q}_{\widetilde{Z}\widetilde{Z}},\left\{\frac{r_{n}}{n}\sum_{i\in I_{n,j}}\widetilde{Z}_{i,j}\varepsilon_{i,j}:j\in[J]\right\},\hat{A}_{r}\right),\hskip 11.74988pt\widehat{S}_{n}=\left(\widehat{Q}_{\widetilde{Z}X},\widehat{Q}_{\widetilde{Z}\widetilde{Z}},\left\{\frac{r_{n}}{n}\sum_{i\in I_{n,j}}\widetilde{Z}_{i,j}\hat{\varepsilon}^{r}_{i,j}:j\in[J]\right\},\hat{A}_{r}\right).

Let
E_{n}
denote the event

E_{n}=I\left\{\text{$\widehat{Q}_{\widetilde{Z}X}$ is of full rank value and $\widehat{Q}_{\widetilde{Z}\widetilde{Z}}$ is invertible}\right\},

and Assumptions
2
-
3
imply that

\liminf_{n\rightarrow\infty}\mathbb{P}\{E_{n}=1\}=1.

Also let
T^{*}_{n}(g)=0
if
E_{n}=0
.

We first give the proof for the Wald statistic based on TSLS.
Note that whenever
E_{n}=1
and
\mathcal{H}_{0}
is true,
the Frisch-Waugh-Lovell theorem implies that

\displaystyle r_{n}T_{n}

\displaystyle=\bigg\|r_{n}\left(\lambda_{\beta}^{\top}\hat{\beta}_{tsls}-\lambda_{0}\right)\bigg\|_{\hat{A}_{r}}=\bigg\|r_{n}\lambda_{\beta}^{\top}\left(\hat{\beta}_{tsls}-\beta_{n}\right)\bigg\|_{\hat{A}_{r}}

\displaystyle=\bigg\|\lambda_{\beta}^{\top}\widehat{Q}^{-1}\widehat{Q}^{\top}_{\widetilde{Z}X}\widehat{Q}_{\widetilde{Z}\widetilde{Z}}^{-1}\sum_{j\in[J]}\frac{r_{n}}{n}\sum_{i\in I_{n,j}}\widetilde{Z}_{i,j}\varepsilon_{i,j}\bigg\|_{\hat{A}_{r}}=T(\iota_{J}S_{n}),

(39)

where
\widehat{Q}=\widehat{Q}^{\top}_{\widetilde{Z}X}\widehat{Q}_{\widetilde{Z}\widetilde{Z}}^{-1}\widehat{Q}_{\widetilde{Z}X}
and
\iota_{J}\in\textbf{G}
is a
J\times 1
vector of ones.

In the following, we divide the proof into three steps. In the first step, we show

\displaystyle T_{n}^{*}(g)=T(g\widehat{S}_{n})+o_{P}(1)\;\;\text{for any $g\in\textbf{G}$}.

(40)

In the second step, we show

\displaystyle T(g\widehat{S}_{n})=T(gS_{n})+o_{P}(1)\;\;\text{for any $g\in\textbf{G}$}.

(41)

In the last step, we prove the desired result.

Step 1.

By the continuous mapping theorem, it suffices to show

\widehat{Q}^{*}_{\widetilde{Z}X}(g)=\widehat{Q}_{\widetilde{Z}X}+o_{P}(1)
. Note that

\displaystyle\widehat{Q}^{*}_{\widetilde{Z}X}(g)=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{j\in[J]}\sum_{i\in I_{n,j}}\widetilde{Z}_{i,j}X_{i,j}^{*\top}(g)=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{j\in[J]}\sum_{i\in I_{n,j}}\widetilde{Z}_{i,j}\left(X_{i,j}+(g_{j}-1)\tilde{v}_{i,j}\right)^{\top}.

Therefore, it suffices to show
\frac{1}{n_{j}}\sum_{i\in I_{n,j}}\widetilde{Z}_{i,j}\tilde{v}_{i,j}=o_{P}(1)
for all
j\in[J]
. Recall
\overline{Z}_{i,j}
is just
\widetilde{Z}_{i,j}
interacted with all the cluster dummies and
\widetilde{\Pi}_{\overline{Z}}
is the OLS coefficient of
\overline{Z}_{i,j}
defined in (
8
). Denote
\widetilde{\Pi}_{\overline{Z},j}
as the
j
-th block of
\widetilde{\Pi}_{\overline{Z}}
, which corresponds to the OLS coefficient of
\widetilde{\Pi}_{\overline{Z},j}
. We have

\displaystyle\frac{1}{n_{j}}\sum_{i\in I_{n,j}}\widetilde{Z}_{i,j}\tilde{v}_{i,j}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{n_{j}}\sum_{i\in I_{n,j}}\left(\widetilde{Z}_{i,j}X_{i,j}-\widetilde{Z}_{i,j}\widetilde{Z}_{i,j}^{\top}\widetilde{\Pi}_{\overline{Z},j}-\widetilde{Z}_{i,j}W_{i,j}^{\top}\widetilde{\Pi}_{w}\right)=\widehat{Q}_{\widetilde{Z}X,j}-\widehat{Q}_{\widetilde{Z}\widetilde{Z},j}\widetilde{\Pi}_{\overline{Z},j}+o_{P}(1),

(42)

where the second equality in (
42
) holds by
\frac{1}{n_{j}}\sum_{i\in I_{n,j}}\widetilde{Z}_{i,j}W_{i,j}^{\top}=o_{P}(1)
and
\widetilde{\Pi}_{w}=O_{P}(1)
.
In particular,

\displaystyle\widetilde{\Pi}_{w}=\left(\widehat{Q}_{\widetilde{W}\widetilde{W}}-\widehat{Q}_{\widetilde{W}\hat{\varepsilon}}\widehat{Q}^{-1}_{\hat{\varepsilon}\hat{\varepsilon}}\widehat{Q}_{\hat{\varepsilon}\widetilde{W}}\right)^{-1}\left(\widehat{Q}_{\widetilde{W}X}-\widehat{Q}_{\widetilde{W}\hat{\varepsilon}}\widehat{Q}^{-1}_{\hat{\varepsilon}\hat{\varepsilon}}\widehat{Q}_{\hat{\varepsilon}X}\right),

where
\widehat{Q}_{\widetilde{W}\widetilde{W}}=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{j\in[J]}\sum_{i\in I_{n,j}}\widetilde{W}_{i,j}\widetilde{W}_{i,j}^{\top}
,

\widehat{Q}_{\widetilde{W}\hat{\varepsilon}}=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{j\in[J]}\sum_{i\in I_{n,j}}\widetilde{W}_{i,j}\hat{\varepsilon}_{i,j}
,

\widehat{Q}_{\hat{\varepsilon}\hat{\varepsilon}}=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{j\in[J]}\sum_{i\in I_{n,j}}\hat{\varepsilon}_{i,j}^{2}
,

\widetilde{W}_{i,j}=W_{i,j}-\widehat{\Gamma}_{w,j}^{\top}\widetilde{Z}_{i,j}
,
and
\widehat{\Gamma}_{w,j}=\widehat{Q}^{-1}_{\widetilde{Z}\widetilde{Z},j}\widehat{Q}_{\widetilde{Z}W,j}
.
Notice that by
\widehat{Q}_{\widetilde{Z}W,j}=o_{P}(1)
,
\widehat{\Gamma}_{w,j}=o_{P}(1)
so that

\displaystyle\widehat{Q}_{\widetilde{W}\widetilde{W}}=\widehat{Q}_{WW}+o_{P}(1).

(43)

Similarly, we have

\displaystyle\widehat{Q}_{\widetilde{W}\hat{\varepsilon}}=\widehat{Q}_{W\hat{\varepsilon}}+o_{P}(1)=o_{P}(1),

(44)

where the second equality follows from
\widehat{Q}_{W\hat{\varepsilon}}=0
by the first-order condition of the
k
-class estimators.
Furthermore,
\widehat{Q}_{\hat{\varepsilon}\hat{\varepsilon}}\geq c>0
by Assumption
2
(iii)
and
\widehat{Q}_{\hat{\varepsilon}\hat{\varepsilon}}\geq\frac{1}{n}\sum_{j\in[J]}\sum_{i\in I_{n,j}}\dot{\varepsilon}^{2}_{i,j}
,
where
\dot{\varepsilon}_{i,j}
is the residual from the cluster-level projection of
\varepsilon_{i,j}
on
W_{i,j}
.
Therefore,
\widehat{Q}_{\widetilde{W}\widetilde{W}}-\widehat{Q}_{\widetilde{W}\hat{\varepsilon}}\widehat{Q}^{-1}_{\hat{\varepsilon}\hat{\varepsilon}}\widehat{Q}_{\hat{\varepsilon}\widetilde{W}}=\widehat{Q}_{WW}+o_{P}(1)
by combining (
43
) and (
44
), and further by Assumption
1
(iv),

\displaystyle\left(\widehat{Q}_{\widetilde{W}\widetilde{W}}-\widehat{Q}_{\widetilde{W}\hat{\varepsilon}}\widehat{Q}^{-1}_{\hat{\varepsilon}\hat{\varepsilon}}\widehat{Q}_{\hat{\varepsilon}\widetilde{W}}\right)^{-1}=O_{P}(1).

(45)

Next, we define

\widehat{Q}_{\widetilde{Z}\dot{X},j}=\frac{1}{n_{j}}\sum_{i\in I_{n,j}}\widetilde{Z}_{i,j}\dot{X}_{i,j}^{\top}
and recall
\widehat{Q}_{\widetilde{Z}\widetilde{Z},j}=\frac{1}{n_{j}}\sum_{i\in I_{n,j}}\widetilde{Z}_{i,j}\widetilde{Z}_{i,j}^{\top}
, where
\dot{X}_{i,j}=X_{i,j}-\widetilde{\Pi}_{w}^{\top}W_{i,j}-\widetilde{\Pi}_{\hat{\varepsilon}}^{\top}\hat{\varepsilon}_{i,j}
, and
\widetilde{\Pi}_{w}
and
\widetilde{\Pi}_{\hat{\varepsilon}}
are the OLS coefficients of
W_{i,j}
and
\hat{\varepsilon}_{i,j}
, respectively, from regressing
X_{i,j}
on
(\overline{Z}^{\top}_{i,j},W^{\top}_{i,j},\hat{\varepsilon}_{i,j})^{\top}
using the entire sample. Then, we have

\displaystyle\widetilde{\Pi}_{\overline{Z},j}

\displaystyle=\widehat{Q}^{-1}_{\widetilde{Z}\widetilde{Z},j}\widehat{Q}_{\widetilde{Z}\dot{X},j}=\widehat{Q}^{-1}_{\widetilde{Z}\widetilde{Z},j}\left[\widehat{Q}_{\widetilde{Z}X,j}-\widehat{Q}_{\widetilde{Z}W,j}\widetilde{\Pi}_{w}-\widehat{Q}_{\widetilde{Z}\hat{\varepsilon},j}\widetilde{\Pi}_{\hat{\varepsilon}}\right]=\widehat{Q}^{-1}_{\widetilde{Z}\widetilde{Z},j}\widehat{Q}_{\widetilde{Z}X,j}+o_{P}(1),

(46)

where we use the facts that
\widehat{Q}_{\widetilde{Z}W,j}=o_{P}(1)
,
\widetilde{\Pi}_{w}=O_{P}(1)
,
\widetilde{\Pi}_{\hat{\varepsilon}}=O_{P}(1)
, and
\widehat{Q}_{\widetilde{Z}\hat{\varepsilon},j}=o_{P}(1)
.
In particular,

\displaystyle\widetilde{\Pi}_{\hat{\varepsilon}}=\left(\widehat{Q}_{\tilde{\varepsilon}\tilde{\varepsilon}}-\widehat{Q}_{\tilde{\varepsilon}W}\widehat{Q}^{-1}_{WW}\widehat{Q}_{W\tilde{\varepsilon}}\right)^{-1}\left(\widehat{Q}_{\tilde{\varepsilon}X}-\widehat{Q}_{\tilde{\varepsilon}W}\widehat{Q}^{-1}_{WW}\widehat{Q}_{WX}\right),

where
\widehat{Q}_{\tilde{\varepsilon}\tilde{\varepsilon}}=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{j\in[J]}\sum_{i\in I_{n,j}}\tilde{\varepsilon}^{2}_{i,j}
,

\widehat{Q}_{\tilde{\varepsilon}W}=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{j\in[J]}\sum_{i\in I_{n,j}}\tilde{\varepsilon}_{i,j}W^{\top}_{i,j}
,

\tilde{\varepsilon}_{i,j}=\hat{\varepsilon}_{i,j}-\widehat{\Gamma}_{\hat{\varepsilon},j}^{\top}\widetilde{Z}_{i,j}
,
and
\widehat{\Gamma}_{\hat{\varepsilon},j}=\widehat{Q}_{\widetilde{Z}\widetilde{Z},j}^{-1}\widehat{Q}_{\widetilde{Z}\hat{\varepsilon},j}
.
Then, by using similar arguments as those for (
43
), (
44
), and (
45
), we obtain

\displaystyle\widehat{Q}_{\tilde{\varepsilon}\tilde{\varepsilon}}=\widehat{Q}_{\hat{\varepsilon}\hat{\varepsilon}}+o_{P}(1),\;\widehat{Q}_{\tilde{\varepsilon}W}=\widehat{Q}_{\hat{\varepsilon}W}+o_{P}(1)=o_{P}(1),\;\text{and}\;\left(\widehat{Q}_{\tilde{\varepsilon}\tilde{\varepsilon}}-\widehat{Q}_{\tilde{\varepsilon}W}\widehat{Q}^{-1}_{WW}\widehat{Q}_{W\tilde{\varepsilon}}\right)^{-1}=O_{P}(1).

To see the last equality in (
46
), we note that

\displaystyle\widehat{Q}_{\widetilde{Z}\hat{\varepsilon},j}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{n_{j}}\sum_{i\in I_{n,j}}\widetilde{Z}_{i,j}\hat{\varepsilon}_{i,j}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{n_{j}}\sum_{i\in I_{n,j}}\widetilde{Z}_{i,j}\varepsilon_{i,j}-\frac{1}{n_{j}}\sum_{i\in I_{n,j}}\widetilde{Z}_{i,j}X_{i,j}^{\top}(\hat{\beta}_{tsls}-\beta_{n})-\frac{1}{n_{j}}\sum_{i\in I_{n,j}}\widetilde{Z}_{i,j}W_{i,j}^{\top}(\hat{\gamma}_{tsls}-\gamma)=o_{P}(1),

where the last equality holds by Assumption
1
(ii), Lemma
12.3
, and Lemma
12.1
.
Plugging (
46
) into (
42
), we obtain the desired result that
\frac{1}{n_{j}}\sum_{i\in I_{n,j}}\widetilde{Z}_{i,j}\tilde{v}_{i,j}=o_{P}(1)
.

Step 2.

We note that whenever
E_{n}=1
, for every
g\in\textbf{G}
,

\displaystyle\left|T(gS_{n})-T(g\widehat{S}_{n})\right|

\displaystyle\leq\bigg\|\lambda_{\beta}^{\top}\widehat{Q}^{-1}\widehat{Q}^{\top}_{\widetilde{Z}X}\widehat{Q}_{\widetilde{Z}\widetilde{Z}}^{-1}\sum_{j\in[J]}\frac{n_{j}}{n}\frac{1}{n_{j}}\sum_{i\in I_{n,j}}g_{j}\widetilde{Z}_{i,j}X_{i,j}^{\top}r_{n}(\beta_{n}-\hat{\beta}^{r}_{tsls})\bigg\|_{\hat{A}_{r}}

\displaystyle+\bigg\|\lambda_{\beta}^{\top}\widehat{Q}^{-1}\widehat{Q}^{\top}_{\widetilde{Z}X}\widehat{Q}_{\widetilde{Z}\widetilde{Z}}^{-1}\sum_{j\in[J]}\frac{n_{j}}{n}\frac{1}{n_{j}}\sum_{i\in I_{n,j}}g_{j}\widetilde{Z}_{i,j}W_{i,j}^{\top}r_{n}(\gamma-\hat{\gamma}^{r}_{tsls})\bigg\|_{\hat{A}_{r}}.

(47)

By Lemma
12.3
,
we have

\displaystyle\limsup_{n\rightarrow\infty}\mathbb{P}\left\{\bigg\|\lambda_{\beta}^{\top}\widehat{Q}^{-1}\widehat{Q}^{\top}_{\widetilde{Z}X}\widehat{Q}_{\widetilde{Z}\widetilde{Z}}^{-1}\sum_{j\in[J]}\frac{n_{j}}{n}\frac{1}{n_{j}}\sum_{i\in I_{n,j}}g_{j}\widetilde{Z}_{i,j}W_{i,j}^{\top}r_{n}(\gamma-\hat{\gamma}^{r}_{tsls})\bigg\|_{\hat{A}_{r}}>\varepsilon;E_{n}=1\right\}=0.

(48)

Note under both cases in Assumption
3
(ii), we have
\sum_{j\in[J]}\xi_{j}a_{j}=1
and

\displaystyle\lambda_{\beta}^{\top}Q^{-1}Q_{\widetilde{Z}X}^{\top}Q_{\widetilde{Z}\widetilde{Z}}^{-1}Q_{\widetilde{Z}X,j}=a_{j}\lambda_{\beta}^{\top}.

(49)

Then, for any
\varepsilon>0
, we have

\displaystyle\limsup_{n\rightarrow\infty}\mathbb{P}\left\{\bigg\|\lambda_{\beta}^{\top}\widehat{Q}^{-1}\widehat{Q}^{\top}_{\widetilde{Z}X}\widehat{Q}_{\widetilde{Z}\widetilde{Z}}^{-1}\sum_{j\in[J]}\frac{n_{j}}{n}\frac{1}{n_{j}}\sum_{i\in I_{n,j}}g_{j}\widetilde{Z}_{i,j}X_{i,j}^{\top}r_{n}(\beta_{n}-\hat{\beta}^{r}_{tsls})\bigg\|_{\hat{A}_{r}}>\varepsilon;E_{n}=1\right\}

\displaystyle=\limsup_{n\rightarrow\infty}\mathbb{P}\left\{\bigg\|\sum_{j\in[J]}\xi_{j}g_{j}a_{j}r_{n}(\lambda_{\beta}^{\top}\beta_{n}-\lambda_{\beta}^{\top}\hat{\beta}^{r}_{tsls})\bigg\|_{\hat{A}_{r}}>\varepsilon;E_{n}=1\right\}=0,

(50)

where the last equality holds because

\lambda_{\beta}^{\top}\hat{\beta}^{r}_{tsls}=\lambda_{0}
under
\mathcal{H}_{0}
.

Note that
T(g\widehat{S}_{n})=T(gS_{n})
whenever
E_{n}=0
as we have defined

T(s)=0
for any
s=\left(s_{1},s_{2},\{s_{3,j}:j\in[J]\},s_{4}\right)
whenever

s_{2}
or
s_{1}^{\top}s_{2}^{-1}s_{1}
is not invertible.
Therefore, results in (
13
), (
48
) and (
13
) imply (
41
).

Step 3.
Note that by
Assumptions
1
,
2
,
4
,
and the continuous mapping theorem, we have

\displaystyle\left(\widehat{Q}_{\widetilde{Z}X},\widehat{Q}_{\widetilde{Z}\widetilde{Z}},\left\{\frac{r_{n}}{n}\sum_{i\in I_{n,j}}\widetilde{Z}_{i,j}\varepsilon_{i,j}:j\in[J]\right\},\hat{A}_{r}\right)\xrightarrow{\hskip 4.46255ptd\hskip 4.46255pt}\left(Q_{\widetilde{Z}X},Q_{\widetilde{Z}\widetilde{Z}},\left\{\mathcal{Z}_{j}:j\in[J]\right\},A_{r}\right)\equiv S,

(51)

where
\xi_{j}>0
for all
j\in[J]
by Assumption
1
(iii).
Therefore, we obtain from (
40
), (
41
), (
51
), and the continuous mapping theorem that

\displaystyle\left(T(S_{n}),\left\{T^{*}_{n}(g):g\in\textbf{G}\right\}\right)\xrightarrow{\hskip 4.46255ptd\hskip 4.46255pt}\left(T(S),\{T(gS):g\in\textbf{G}\}\right).

For any
x\in\textbf{R}
letting
\lceil x\rceil
denote the smallest integer larger than
x
and

k^{*}\equiv\lceil|\textbf{G}|(1-\alpha)\rceil
,
we obtain from (
13
) that

\displaystyle 1\left\{T_{n}>\hat{c}_{n}(1-\alpha)\right\}=1\left\{T(S_{n})>T_{n}^{*(k^{*})}(\textbf{G})\right\}.

(52)

Since
\liminf_{n\rightarrow\infty}P\{E_{n}=1\}=1
, we have

\displaystyle\limsup_{n\rightarrow\infty}\mathbb{P}\{T_{n}>\hat{c}_{n}(1-\alpha))\}=\limsup_{n\rightarrow\infty}\mathbb{P}\{T_{n}>\hat{c}_{n}(1-\alpha));E_{n}=1\}

\displaystyle\leq\limsup_{n\rightarrow\infty}\mathbb{P}\{T(S_{n})\geq T_{n}^{*(k^{*})}(\textbf{G});E_{n}=1\}\leq\mathbb{P}\{T(S)\geq T^{(k^{*})}(S|\textbf{G})\}\leq\alpha+2^{1-q},

where the second inequality is due to the Portmanteau’s theorem.
To see the last inequality, we note that for all
g\in\textbf{G}
,
\mathbb{P}\{T(gS)=T(-gS)\}=1
,

\mathbb{P}\{T(gS)=T(\tilde{g}S)\}=0
for
\tilde{g}\notin\{g,-g\}
,
and under the null,
T(gS)
has the same distribution across
g\in\textbf{G}
. Let
|\textbf{G}|=2^{q}
. Then, we have

\displaystyle|\textbf{G}|\mathbb{E}1\{T(S)\geq T^{(k^{*})}(S|\textbf{G})\}=\mathbb{E}\sum_{g\in\textbf{G}}1\{T(gS)\geq T^{(k^{*})}(S|\textbf{G})\}\leq|\textbf{G}|-k^{*}+2,

which implies

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}1\{T(S)\geq T^{(k^{*})}(S|\textbf{G})\}\leq 1-\frac{k^{*}}{|\textbf{G}|}+\frac{2}{|\textbf{G}|}\leq\alpha+\frac{1}{2^{J-1}}.

For the lower bound, first note that
k^{*}>|\textbf{G}|-2

implies that
\alpha-\frac{1}{2^{J-1}}\leq 0
, in which case the result trivially follows.
Now assume
k^{*}\leq|\textbf{G}|-2
, then

\displaystyle\liminf_{n\rightarrow\infty}\mathbb{P}\{T_{n}>\hat{c}_{n}(1-\alpha)\}=\liminf_{n\rightarrow\infty}\mathbb{P}\{T(S_{n})>T_{n}^{*(k^{*})}(\textbf{G})\}\geq\mathbb{P}\{T(S)>T^{(k^{*})}(S|\textbf{G})\}

\displaystyle\geq\mathbb{P}\{T(S)>T^{(k^{*}+2)}(S|\textbf{G})\}+\mathbb{P}\{T(S)=T^{(k^{*}+2)}(S|\textbf{G})\}\geq\alpha-\frac{1}{2^{J-1}},

where the first equality follows from (
52
),
the first inequality follows from Portmanteau’s theorem,
the second inequality holds because

\mathbb{P}\{T^{(\textbf{z}+2)}(S|\textbf{G})>T^{(\textbf{z})}(S|\textbf{G})\}=1

for any integer
\textbf{z}\leq|\textbf{G}|-2

by (
38
) and Assumption
1
,
and the last inequality follows from noticing that
k^{*}+2=\lceil|\textbf{G}|((1-\alpha)+2/|\textbf{G}|)\rceil=\lceil|\textbf{G}|(1-\alpha^{\prime})\rceil
with
\alpha^{\prime}=\alpha-\frac{1}{2^{J-1}}

and the properties of randomization tests.

Lemma
12.1
and
12.2
in the Supplement further show the other
k
-class estimators and their null-restricted and bootstrap counterparts are asymptotically equivalent to those of the TSLS estimator. Therefore, the results for LIML, FULL, and BA estimators can be derived in the same manner.
\blacksquare

14
Proof of Theorem
4.2

For the power of the
T_{n}
-based wild bootstrap test, we focus on the TSLS estimator. As shown in the end of the proof of Theorem
4.1
, the test statistics constructed using LIML, FULL, and BA estimators and their bootstrap counterparts are asymptotically equivalent to those constructed based on TSLS estimator, which leads to the desired result. Note that

\displaystyle||r_{n}(\lambda_{\beta}^{\top}\hat{\beta}_{tsls}-\lambda_{0})||_{\hat{A}_{r}}

\displaystyle=||r_{n}\lambda_{\beta}^{\top}(\hat{\beta}_{tsls}-\beta_{n})+r_{n}\lambda_{\beta}^{\top}(\beta_{n}-\hat{\beta}^{r}_{tsls})||_{\hat{A}_{r}}

\displaystyle=\Big\|\lambda_{\beta}^{\top}\widehat{Q}^{-1}\widehat{Q}_{\widetilde{Z}X}^{\top}\widehat{Q}_{\widetilde{Z}\widetilde{Z}}^{-1}\sum_{j\in[J]}\sum_{i\in I_{n,j}}\frac{r_{n}\widetilde{Z}_{i,j}\varepsilon_{i,j}}{n}+r_{n}\lambda_{\beta}^{\top}(\beta_{n}-\hat{\beta}^{r}_{tsls})\Big\|_{\hat{A}_{r}}

\displaystyle=\Big\|\lambda_{\beta}^{\top}\widehat{Q}^{-1}\widehat{Q}_{\widetilde{Z}X}^{\top}\widehat{Q}_{\widetilde{Z}\widetilde{Z}}^{-1}\sum_{j\in[J]}\sum_{i\in I_{n,j}}\left(\frac{r_{n}\widetilde{Z}_{i,j}\varepsilon_{i,j}}{n}+\frac{\widetilde{Z}_{i,j}X_{i,j}^{\top}}{n}r_{n}(\beta_{n}-\hat{\beta}^{r}_{tsls})\right)\Big\|_{\hat{A}_{r}}.

Notice that Assumptions
1
,
2
,
3
(i), and Lemma
12.1
imply that

r_{n}(\hat{\beta}^{r}_{tsls}-\beta_{n})
is bounded in probability. This implies

\displaystyle r_{n}T_{n}=||r_{n}(\lambda_{\beta}^{\top}\hat{\beta}_{tsls}-\lambda_{0})||_{\hat{A}_{r}}\xrightarrow{\hskip 4.46255ptd\hskip 4.46255pt}\Big\|\sum_{j\in[J]}\left[\lambda_{\beta}^{\top}Q^{-1}Q_{\widetilde{Z}X}^{\top}Q_{\widetilde{Z}\widetilde{Z}}^{-1}\mathcal{Z}_{j}\right]+\mu\Big\|_{A_{r}}.

(53)

Recall
\widehat{Q}
and
\widehat{Q}^{*}_{g}
defined in (
5
) and (
11
), respectively. We have
\widehat{Q}^{*-1}_{g}=\widehat{Q}^{-1}+o_{P}(1)
and

\displaystyle||r_{n}\lambda_{\beta}^{\top}(\hat{\beta}^{*}_{tsls,g}-\hat{\beta}^{r}_{tsls})||_{\hat{A}_{r}}

\displaystyle=\Big\|\lambda_{\beta}^{\top}\widehat{Q}^{*-1}_{g}\widehat{Q}_{\widetilde{Z}X}^{*\top}(g)\widehat{Q}_{\widetilde{Z}\widetilde{Z}}^{-1}\sum_{j\in[J]}\sum_{i\in I_{n,j}}g_{j}\left(\frac{r_{n}\widetilde{Z}_{i,j}\varepsilon_{i,j}}{n}+\frac{\widetilde{Z}_{i,j}X_{i,j}^{\top}}{n}r_{n}(\beta_{n}-\hat{\beta}^{r}_{tsls})+\frac{\widetilde{Z}_{i,j}W_{i,j}^{\top}}{n}r_{n}(\gamma-\hat{\gamma}^{r}_{tsls})\right)\Big\|_{\hat{A}_{r}}

\displaystyle=\Big\|\lambda_{\beta}^{\top}\widehat{Q}^{-1}\widehat{Q}_{\widetilde{Z}X}^{\top}\widehat{Q}_{\widetilde{Z}\widetilde{Z}}^{-1}\sum_{j\in[J]}\sum_{i\in I_{n,j}}g_{j}\left(\frac{r_{n}\widetilde{Z}_{i,j}\varepsilon_{i,j}}{n}+\frac{\widetilde{Z}_{i,j}X_{i,j}^{\top}}{n}r_{n}(\beta_{n}-\hat{\beta}^{r}_{tsls})\right)\Big\|_{\hat{A}_{r}}+o_{P}(1),

(54)

where the last equality follows from Lemma
12.3

and
\widehat{Q}^{*}_{\widetilde{Z}X}(g)=\widehat{Q}_{\widetilde{Z}X}+o_{P}(1)
. Furthermore, we notice that

\displaystyle\hat{\beta}^{r}_{tsls}

\displaystyle=\hat{\beta}_{tsls}-\widehat{Q}^{-1}\lambda_{\beta}\left(\lambda_{\beta}^{\top}\widehat{Q}^{-1}\lambda_{\beta}\right)^{-1}\left(\lambda_{\beta}^{\top}\hat{\beta}_{tsls}-\lambda_{0}\right)

\displaystyle=\hat{\beta}_{tsls}-\widehat{Q}^{-1}\lambda_{\beta}\left\{\left(\lambda_{\beta}^{\top}\widehat{Q}^{-1}\lambda_{\beta}\right)^{-1}\lambda_{\beta}^{\top}(\hat{\beta}_{tsls}-\beta_{n})+\left(\lambda_{\beta}^{\top}\widehat{Q}^{-1}\lambda_{\beta}\right)^{-1}(\lambda_{\beta}^{\top}\beta_{n}-\lambda_{0})\right\}.

(55)

Therefore, employing (
14
) with
r_{n}(\lambda_{\beta}^{\top}\beta_{n}-\lambda_{0})=\lambda_{\beta}^{\top}\mu_{\beta}
,
we conclude that whenever
E_{n}=1
,

\displaystyle\sum_{i\in I_{n,j}}\frac{\widetilde{Z}_{i,j}X_{i,j}^{\top}}{n}r_{n}(\beta_{n}-\hat{\beta}^{r}_{tsls})

\displaystyle=\sum_{i\in I_{n,j}}\frac{\widetilde{Z}_{i,j}X_{i,j}^{\top}}{n}\left\{\left(I_{d_{x}}-\widehat{Q}^{-1}\lambda_{\beta}\left(\lambda_{\beta}^{\top}\widehat{Q}^{-1}\lambda_{\beta}\right)^{-1}\lambda_{\beta}^{\top}\right)r_{n}(\beta_{n}-\hat{\beta}_{tsls})\right.

\displaystyle\left.+\widehat{Q}^{-1}\lambda_{\beta}\left(\lambda_{\beta}^{\top}\widehat{Q}^{-1}\lambda_{\beta}\right)^{-1}\lambda_{\beta}^{\top}\mu_{\beta}\right\}.

Together with
(\ref{eq: boot-unstud-power-2})
, this implies that

\displaystyle\limsup_{n\rightarrow\infty}\mathbb{P}\begin{pmatrix}\Big\|r_{n}\lambda_{\beta}^{\top}(\hat{\beta}^{*}_{tsls,g}-\hat{\beta}^{r}_{tsls})-\lambda_{\beta}^{\top}\widehat{Q}^{-1}\widehat{Q}_{\widetilde{Z}X}^{\top}\widehat{Q}_{\widetilde{Z}\widetilde{Z}}^{-1}\\
\times\sum_{j\in[J]}\sum_{i\in I_{n,j}}g_{j}\left(\frac{r_{n}\widetilde{Z}_{i,j}\varepsilon_{i,j}}{n}+\xi_{j}\widehat{Q}_{\widetilde{Z}X,j}\widehat{Q}^{-1}\lambda_{\beta}\left(\lambda_{\beta}^{\top}\widehat{Q}^{-1}\lambda_{\beta}\right)^{-1}\lambda_{\beta}^{\top}\mu_{\beta}\right)\Big\|_{\hat{A}_{r}}>\varepsilon;E_{n}=1\end{pmatrix}

\displaystyle=\limsup_{n\rightarrow\infty}\mathbb{P}\begin{pmatrix}&\Big\|r_{n}\lambda_{\beta}^{\top}(\hat{\beta}^{*}_{tsls,g}-\hat{\beta}^{r}_{tsls})-\lambda_{\beta}^{\top}Q^{-1}Q_{\widetilde{Z}X}^{\top}Q_{\widetilde{Z}\widetilde{Z}}^{-1}\\
&\times\sum_{j\in[J]}g_{j}\left[\mathcal{Z}_{j}+\xi_{j}Q_{\widetilde{Z}X,j}Q^{-1}\lambda_{\beta}\left(\lambda_{\beta}^{\top}Q^{-1}\lambda_{\beta}\right)^{-1}\mu\right]\Big\|_{\hat{A}_{r}}>\varepsilon;E_{n}=1\end{pmatrix}

\displaystyle=\limsup_{n\rightarrow\infty}\mathbb{P}\begin{pmatrix}&\Big\|r_{n}\lambda_{\beta}^{\top}(\hat{\beta}^{*}_{tsls,g}-\hat{\beta}^{r}_{tsls})-\sum_{j\in[J]}g_{j}\left[\lambda_{\beta}^{\top}Q^{-1}Q_{\widetilde{Z}X}^{\top}Q_{\widetilde{Z}\widetilde{Z}}^{-1}\mathcal{Z}_{j}+\xi_{j}a_{j}\mu\right]\Big\|_{\hat{A}_{r}}>\varepsilon;E_{n}=1\end{pmatrix}=0.

This implies

\displaystyle r_{n}T_{n}^{*}(g)=\Big\|r_{n}\lambda_{\beta}^{\top}(\hat{\beta}^{*}_{tsls,g}-\hat{\beta}^{r}_{tsls})\Big\|_{\hat{A}_{r}}\xrightarrow{\hskip 4.46255ptd\hskip 4.46255pt}\Big\|\sum_{j\in[J]}g_{j}\left[\lambda_{\beta}^{\top}Q^{-1}Q_{\widetilde{Z}X}^{\top}Q_{\widetilde{Z}\widetilde{Z}}^{-1}\mathcal{Z}_{j}+\xi_{j}a_{j}\mu\right]\Big\|_{A_{r}}.

(56)

In addition, let
\textbf{G}_{s}=\textbf{G}\backslash\textbf{G}_{w}
, where
\textbf{G}_{w}=\{g\in\textbf{G}:g_{j}=g_{j^{\prime}},\forall j,j^{\prime}\in\mathcal{J}_{s}\}
.
To establish the power result for the bootstrap test, we request that
\hat{c}_{n}(1-\alpha)
does not take values of
T^{*}_{n}(g)
for
g\in\textbf{G}_{w}
because otherwise the test statistic and the critical value are asymptotically equivalent even under the alternative.
We note that the following condition is imposed in the theorem:
|\textbf{G}_{s}|=|\textbf{G}|-2^{J-J_{s}+1}\geq k^{*}
.
Therefore, based on (
53
) and (
56
), to establish the desired result, it suffices to show that as
||\mu||_{2}\rightarrow\infty
,

\displaystyle\liminf_{n\rightarrow\infty}\mathbb{P}\{T_{n}>\max_{g\in\textbf{G}_{s}}T^{*}_{n}(g)\}\rightarrow 1,

which follows under similar arguments as those employed in the proof of Theorem 3.2 in
Canay
et al. 2021
.
\blacksquare

15
Proof of Theorem
5.1

Following the same argument in the proof of Theorem
5.2
, we can show that

\displaystyle(\sqrt{n}T_{CR,n},\{\sqrt{n}T_{CR,n}^{*}(g)\}_{g\in\textbf{G}})\xrightarrow{\hskip 4.46255ptd\hskip 4.46255pt}(T_{CR,\infty}(\iota_{J}),\{T_{CR,\infty}(g)\}_{g\in\textbf{G}})

where
T_{CR,n}(g)
is defined in the proof of Theorem
5.2
with
\mu=0
as we are under the null.
Then, the rest of the proof is similar to Step 3 in the proof of Theorem
4.1
.
We omit the detail for brevity.
\blacksquare

16
Proof of Theorem
5.2

For the power analysis, we focus on the TSLS estimator. The results for other
k
-class estimators can be derived in the same manner given Lemma
12.2
. Recall
a_{j}
defined in Assumption
3
. We further define

\displaystyle T_{CR,\infty}(g)=\left\|\lambda_{\beta}^{\top}\tilde{Q}\left[\sum_{j\in[J]}g_{j}\mathcal{Z}_{j}\right]+c_{0,g}\mu\right\|_{A_{r,CR,g}},

(57)

where
\tilde{Q}=Q^{-1}Q_{\widetilde{Z}X}^{\top}Q_{\widetilde{Z}\widetilde{Z}}^{-1}
,
Q=Q_{\widetilde{Z}X}^{\top}Q_{\widetilde{Z}\widetilde{Z}}Q_{\widetilde{Z}X}
,

c_{0,g}=\sum_{j\in[J]}\xi_{j}g_{j}a_{j}
, and

\displaystyle A_{r,CR,g}=\begin{pmatrix}\sum_{j\in[J]}\left\{\lambda_{\beta}^{\top}\tilde{Q}\left[g_{j}\mathcal{Z}_{j}-a_{j}\xi_{j}\sum_{\tilde{j}\in[J]}g_{\tilde{j}}\mathcal{Z}_{\tilde{j}}\right]+\xi_{j}(g_{j}-c_{0,g})a_{j}\mu\right\}\\
\times\left\{\lambda_{\beta}^{\top}\tilde{Q}\left[g_{j}\mathcal{Z}_{j}-a_{j}\xi_{j}\sum_{\tilde{j}\in[J]}g_{\tilde{j}}\mathcal{Z}_{\tilde{j}}\right]+\xi_{j}(g_{j}-c_{0,g})a_{j}\mu\right\}^{\top}\end{pmatrix}^{-1}.

We order
\{T_{CR,\infty}(g)\}_{g\in\textbf{G}}
in ascending order:

(T_{CR,\infty})^{(1)}\leq\cdots\leq(T_{CR,\infty})^{|\textbf{G}|}
. In the proof of Theorem
4.2
, we have already shown that, under
\mathcal{H}_{1,n}
,

\displaystyle r_{n}(\lambda_{\beta}^{\top}\hat{\beta}_{tsls}-\lambda_{0})\xrightarrow{\hskip 4.46255ptd\hskip 4.46255pt}\sum_{j\in[J]}\left[\lambda_{\beta}^{\top}\tilde{Q}\mathcal{Z}_{j}\right]+\mu,\hskip 11.74988ptr_{n}(\lambda_{\beta}^{\top}\hat{\beta}^{*}_{tsls,g}-\lambda_{0})\xrightarrow{\hskip 4.46255ptd\hskip 4.46255pt}\sum_{j\in[J]}g_{j}\left[\lambda_{\beta}^{\top}\tilde{Q}\mathcal{Z}_{j}\right]+c_{0,g}\mu.

Next, we derive the limit of
\hat{A}_{r,CR}
and
\hat{A}^{*}_{r,CR,g}
. We first note that

\displaystyle\frac{r_{n}}{n}\sum_{i\in I_{n,j}}\widetilde{Z}_{i,j}\hat{\varepsilon}_{i,j}

\displaystyle=\frac{r_{n}}{n}\sum_{i\in I_{n,j}}\widetilde{Z}_{i,j}\left[\varepsilon_{i,j}-X_{i,j}^{\top}(\hat{\beta}_{tsls}-\beta_{n})-W_{i,j}^{\top}(\hat{\gamma}_{tsls}-\gamma)\right]

\displaystyle=\frac{r_{n}}{n}\sum_{i\in I_{n,j}}\widetilde{Z}_{i,j}\varepsilon_{i,j}-\xi_{j}\widehat{Q}_{\widetilde{Z}X,j}r_{n}(\hat{\beta}_{tsls}-\beta_{n})+o_{P}(1)

\displaystyle=\frac{r_{n}}{n}\sum_{i\in I_{n,j}}\widetilde{Z}_{i,j}\varepsilon_{i,j}-\xi_{j}\widehat{Q}_{\widetilde{Z}X,j}\widehat{Q}^{-1}\widehat{Q}_{\widetilde{Z}X}^{\top}\widehat{Q}_{\widetilde{Z}\widetilde{Z}}^{-1}\sum_{\tilde{j}\in[J]}\frac{r_{n}}{n}\sum_{i\in I_{n,\tilde{j}}}\widetilde{Z}_{i,\tilde{j}}\varepsilon_{i,\tilde{j}}+o_{P}(1)

\displaystyle\xrightarrow{\hskip 4.46255ptd\hskip 4.46255pt}\mathcal{Z}_{j}-\xi_{j}Q_{\widetilde{Z}X,j}Q^{-1}Q_{\widetilde{Z}X}^{\top}Q_{\widetilde{Z}\widetilde{Z}}^{-1}\sum_{\tilde{j}\in[J]}\mathcal{Z}_{\tilde{j}}+o_{P}(1),

where the first equality holds by Lemma
12.3
. This implies

\displaystyle\frac{r_{n}^{2}}{n}\hat{\Omega}_{CR}\xrightarrow{\hskip 4.46255ptd\hskip 4.46255pt}\sum_{j\in[J]}\left(\mathcal{Z}_{j}-\xi_{j}Q_{\widetilde{Z}X,j}\tilde{Q}\sum_{\tilde{j}\in[J]}\mathcal{Z}_{\tilde{j}}\right)\left(\mathcal{Z}_{j}-\xi_{j}Q_{\widetilde{Z}X,j}\tilde{Q}\sum_{\tilde{j}\in[J]}\mathcal{Z}_{\tilde{j}}\right)^{\top},

\displaystyle\frac{r_{n}^{2}}{n}\hat{A}_{r,CR}\xrightarrow{\hskip 4.46255ptd\hskip 4.46255pt}A_{r,CR,\iota_{J}},\hskip 11.74988pt\text{and}\hskip 11.74988ptT_{CR,n}\xrightarrow{\hskip 4.46255ptd\hskip 4.46255pt}T_{CR,\infty}(\iota_{J}),

where
\iota_{J}
is a
J\times 1
vector of ones. Similarly, we have

\displaystyle\frac{r_{n}}{n}\sum_{i\in I_{n,j}}\widetilde{Z}_{i,j}\hat{\varepsilon}^{*}_{i,j}(g)

\displaystyle=\frac{r_{n}}{n}\sum_{i\in I_{n,j}}\widetilde{Z}_{i,j}\left[g_{j}\hat{\varepsilon}_{i,j}^{r}-X_{i,j}^{*\top}(g)(\hat{\beta}_{tsls,g}^{*}-\hat{\beta}^{r}_{tsls})-W_{i,j}^{\top}(\hat{\gamma}_{tsls,g}^{*}-\hat{\gamma}^{r}_{tsls})\right]

\displaystyle=\frac{g_{j}r_{n}}{n}\sum_{i\in I_{n,j}}\widetilde{Z}_{i,j}\hat{\varepsilon}_{i,j}^{r}-\xi_{j}\widehat{Q}_{\widetilde{Z}X,j}^{*}(g)r_{n}(\hat{\beta}_{tsls,g}^{*}-\hat{\beta}^{r}_{tsls})+o_{P}(1)

\displaystyle=\frac{g_{j}r_{n}}{n}\sum_{i\in I_{n,j}}\widetilde{Z}_{i,j}\varepsilon_{i,j}-\xi_{j}g_{j}\widehat{Q}_{\widetilde{Z}X,j}r_{n}(\hat{\beta}^{r}_{tsls}-\beta_{n})-\xi_{j}\widehat{Q}_{\widetilde{Z}X,j}r_{n}(\hat{\beta}_{tsls,g}^{*}-\hat{\beta}^{r}_{tsls})+o_{P}(1)

\displaystyle=\frac{g_{j}r_{n}}{n}\sum_{i\in I_{n,j}}\widetilde{Z}_{i,j}\varepsilon_{i,j}-\xi_{j}g_{j}Q_{\widetilde{Z}X,j}r_{n}(\hat{\beta}^{r}_{tsls}-\beta_{n})-\xi_{j}Q_{\widetilde{Z}X,j}r_{n}(\hat{\beta}_{tsls,g}^{*}-\hat{\beta}^{r}_{tsls})+o_{P}(1),

(58)

where
\widehat{Q}_{\widetilde{Z}X,j}^{*}(g)=\frac{1}{n_{j}}\sum_{i\in I_{n,j}}\widetilde{Z}_{i,j}X_{i,j}^{*\top}(g)
, the second equality is by Lemma
12.3
, and the last equality holds because
\widehat{Q}_{\widetilde{Z}X,j}=Q_{\widetilde{Z}X,j}+o_{P}(1)
,
\widehat{Q}_{\widetilde{Z}X,j}^{*}(g)=Q_{\widetilde{Z}X,j}+o_{P}(1)
proved in Step 1 of the proof of Theorem
4.1
,
r_{n}(\hat{\beta}^{r}_{tsls}-\beta_{n})=O_{P}(1)
, and
r_{n}(\hat{\beta}^{*}_{tsls,g}-\beta_{n})=O_{P}(1)
. In addition, following the same arguments that lead to (
14
), we have

\displaystyle r_{n}(\hat{\beta}_{tsls,g}^{*}-\hat{\beta}^{r}_{tsls})

\displaystyle=\tilde{Q}\sum_{\tilde{j}\in[J]}\sum_{i\in I_{n,\tilde{j}}}\frac{r_{n}g_{\tilde{j}}\widetilde{Z}_{i,\tilde{j}}\varepsilon_{i,\tilde{j}}}{n}+\tilde{Q}\sum_{\tilde{j}\in[J]}\xi_{\tilde{j}}g_{\tilde{j}}Q_{\widetilde{Z}X,\tilde{j}}r_{n}(\beta_{n}-\hat{\beta}^{r}_{tsls})+o_{P}(1)

\displaystyle=\tilde{Q}\sum_{\tilde{j}\in[J]}\sum_{i\in I_{n,\tilde{j}}}\frac{r_{n}g_{\tilde{j}}\widetilde{Z}_{i,\tilde{j}}\varepsilon_{i,\tilde{j}}}{n}+\sum_{\tilde{j}\in[J]}\xi_{\tilde{j}}g_{\tilde{j}}a_{\tilde{j}}r_{n}(\beta_{n}-\hat{\beta}^{r}_{tsls})+o_{P}(1).

(59)

Note
\widehat{Q}^{*-1}_{g}\widehat{Q}_{\widetilde{Z}X}^{*\top}(g)\widehat{Q}_{\widetilde{Z}\widetilde{Z}}^{-1}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle p}}{{\longrightarrow}}\tilde{Q}
. Therefore, combining (
58
) and (
59
), we have

\displaystyle\lambda_{\beta}^{\top}\widehat{Q}_{g}^{*-1}\widehat{Q}_{\widetilde{Z}X}^{*\top}(g)\widehat{Q}_{\widetilde{Z}\widetilde{Z}}^{-1}\left[\frac{r_{n}}{n}\sum_{i\in I_{n,j}}\widetilde{Z}_{i,j}\hat{\varepsilon}^{*}_{i,j}(g)\right]

\displaystyle=\lambda_{\beta}^{\top}\tilde{Q}\left[\frac{g_{j}r_{n}}{n}\sum_{i\in I_{n,j}}\widetilde{Z}_{i,j}\varepsilon_{i,j}-\xi_{j}g_{j}Q_{\widetilde{Z}X,j}r_{n}(\hat{\beta}^{r}_{tsls}-\beta_{n})-\xi_{j}Q_{\widetilde{Z}X,j}r_{n}(\hat{\beta}_{tsls,g}^{*}-\hat{\beta}^{r}_{tsls})\right]+o_{P}(1)

\displaystyle=\lambda_{\beta}^{\top}\tilde{Q}\left[\frac{g_{j}r_{n}}{n}\sum_{i\in I_{n,j}}\widetilde{Z}_{i,j}\varepsilon_{i,j}\right]-\xi_{j}g_{j}a_{j}\lambda_{\beta}^{\top}r_{n}(\hat{\beta}^{r}_{tsls}-\beta_{n})-\xi_{j}a_{j}\lambda_{\beta}^{\top}r_{n}(\hat{\beta}_{tsls,g}^{*}-\hat{\beta}^{r}_{tsls})+o_{P}(1)

\displaystyle\xrightarrow{\hskip 4.46255ptd\hskip 4.46255pt}\lambda_{\beta}^{\top}\tilde{Q}g_{j}\mathcal{Z}_{j}-\xi_{j}a_{j}\lambda_{\beta}^{\top}\tilde{Q}\sum_{\tilde{j}\in[J]}g_{\tilde{j}}\mathcal{Z}_{\tilde{j}}+\xi_{j}(g_{j}-c_{0,g})a_{j}\mu,

where the second equality is by the fact that
\tilde{Q}Q_{\widetilde{Z}X,j}=a_{j}I_{d_{x}}
and the last convergence is by the fact that
\lambda_{\beta}^{\top}r_{n}(\hat{\beta}^{r}_{tsls}-\beta_{n})=-\mu
. This implies
