\displaystyle\frac{r_{n}^{2}}{n}\hat{A}_{r,CR,g}^{*}\xrightarrow{\hskip 4.46255ptd\hskip 4.46255pt}A_{r,CR,g},\hskip 11.74988pt\text{and thus,}\hskip 11.74988pt(\sqrt{n}T_{CR,n},\{\sqrt{n}T_{CR,n}^{*}(g)\}_{g\in\textbf{G}})\xrightarrow{\hskip 4.46255ptd\hskip 4.46255pt}(T_{CR,\infty}(\iota_{J}),\{T_{CR,\infty}(g)\}_{g\in\textbf{G}}).

By the Portmanteau theorem, we have

\displaystyle\liminf_{n\rightarrow\infty}\mathbb{P}\{T_{CR,n}>\hat{c}_{CR,n}(1-\alpha)\}

\displaystyle\geq\mathbb{P}\left\{T_{CR,\infty}(\iota_{J})>(T_{CR,\infty})^{(k^{*})}\right\}.

We aim to show that, as
||\mu||_{2}\rightarrow\infty
, we have

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\left\{T_{CR,\infty}(\iota_{J})>\max_{g\in\textbf{G}_{s}}T_{CR,\infty}(g)\right\}\rightarrow 1,

(60)

where
\textbf{G}_{s}=\textbf{G}\backslash\textbf{G}_{w}
, and
\textbf{G}_{w}=\{g\in\textbf{G}:g_{j}=g_{j^{\prime}},\forall j,j^{\prime}\in\mathcal{J}_{s}\}
.
Then, given
|\textbf{G}_{s}|=|\textbf{G}|-2^{J-J_{s}+1}
and
k^{*}=\lceil|\textbf{G}|(1-\alpha)\rceil\leq|\textbf{G}|-2^{J-J_{s}+1}
, (
60
) implies that as
||\mu||_{2}\rightarrow\infty
,

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\left\{T_{CR,\infty}(\iota_{J})>(T_{CR,\infty})^{(k^{*})}\right\}\geq\mathbb{P}\left\{T_{CR,\infty}(\iota_{J})>\max_{g\in\textbf{G}_{s}}T_{CR,\infty}(g)\right\}\rightarrow 1.

Therefore, it suffices to establish (
60
).

By (
57
), we see that

T_{CR,\infty}(\iota_{J})=\left\|\lambda_{\beta}^{\top}\tilde{Q}\left[\sum_{j\in[J]}\mathcal{Z}_{j}\right]+\mu\right\|_{A_{r,CR,\iota_{J}}},

and
A_{r,CR,\iota_{J}}
is independent of
\mu
as
c_{0,\iota_{J}}=1
. In addition, we have

\lambda_{\min}(\tilde{Q}^{\top}\lambda_{\beta}^{\top}A_{r,CR,\iota_{J}}\lambda_{\beta}\tilde{Q})>0
with probability one.
Therefore, for any
\delta>0
, we can find a sufficiently small constant
c>0
and a sufficiently large constant
M>0
such that with probability greater than
1-\delta
, for any
\mu
,

\displaystyle T_{CR,\infty}(\iota_{J})\geq c||\mu||^{2}_{2}-M.

(61)

On the other hand, for
g\in\textbf{G}_{s}
,
we can write
T_{CR,\infty}(g)
as

\displaystyle T_{CR,\infty}(g)

\displaystyle=\biggl\{(N_{0,g}+c_{0,g}\mu)^{\top}\left[\sum_{j\in[J]}(N_{j,g}+c_{j,g}\mu)(N_{j,g}+c_{j,g}\mu)^{\top}\right]^{-1}(N_{0,g}+c_{0,g}\mu)\biggr\},

where for
j\in[J]
,

N_{0,g}=\lambda_{\beta}^{\top}\tilde{Q}\left[\sum_{j\in[J]}g_{j}\mathcal{Z}_{j}\right]
,

N_{j,g}=\lambda_{\beta}^{\top}\tilde{Q}\left[g_{j}\mathcal{Z}_{j}-a_{j}\xi_{j}\sum_{\tilde{j}\in[J]}g_{\tilde{j}}\mathcal{Z}_{\tilde{j}}\right]
,
and
c_{j,g}=\xi_{j}(g_{j}-c_{0,g})a_{j}
.

We claim that for
g\in\textbf{G}_{s}
,
c_{j,g}\neq 0
for some
j\in\mathcal{J}_{s}
. Suppose it does not hold, then it implies that
g_{j}=c_{0,g}
for all
j\in\mathcal{J}_{s}
, i.e.,
for all
j\in\mathcal{J}_{s}
,
g_{j}
shares the same sign, and thus, contradicts the definition of
\textbf{G}_{s}
. Therefore, combining the claim with the assumption that
\min_{j\in\mathcal{J}_{s}}|a_{j}|>0
, we have

\min_{g\in\textbf{G}_{s}}\sum_{j\in[J]}c_{j,g}^{2}>0.

In addition, we note that

\displaystyle\sum_{j\in[J]}(N_{j,g}+c_{j,g}\mu)(N_{j,g}+c_{j,g}\mu)^{\top}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{j\in[J]}N_{j,g}N_{j,g}^{\top}+\sum_{j\in[J]}c_{j,g}N_{j,g}\mu^{\top}+\sum_{j\in[J]}c_{j,g}\mu N_{j,g}^{\top}+(\sum_{j\in[J]}c_{j,g}^{2})\mu\mu^{\top}

\displaystyle\equiv

\displaystyle M_{1}+M_{2}\mu^{\top}+\mu M_{2}^{\top}+\overline{c}^{2}\mu\mu^{\top},

where we denote

M_{1}=\sum_{j\in[J]}N_{j,g}N_{j,g}^{\top}
,

M_{2}=\sum_{j\in[J]}c_{j,g}N_{j,g}
, and
\overline{c}^{2}=\sum_{j\in[J]}c_{j,g}^{2}
.
For notation ease, we suppress the dependence of
(M_{1},M_{2},\overline{c})
on
g
.
Then, we have

\displaystyle M_{1}+M_{2}\mu^{\top}+\mu M_{2}^{\top}+\overline{c}^{2}\mu\mu^{\top}=M_{1}-\frac{M_{2}M_{2}^{\top}}{\overline{c}^{2}}+\left(\frac{M_{2}}{\overline{c}}+\overline{c}\mu\right)\left(\frac{M_{2}}{\overline{c}}+\overline{c}\mu\right)^{\top}.

Note for any
d_{r}\times 1
vector
u
, by the Cauchy–Schwarz inequality,

\displaystyle u^{\top}\left(M_{1}-\frac{M_{2}M_{2}^{\top}}{\overline{c}^{2}}\right)u=\sum_{j\in[J]}(u^{\top}N_{j,g})^{2}-\frac{\left(\sum_{j\in[J]}u^{\top}N_{j,g}c_{j,g}\right)^{2}}{\sum_{j\in[J]}c_{j,g}^{2}}\geq 0,

where the equal sign holds if and only if there exist
(u,g,c)\in\Re^{d_{r}}\times\textbf{G}_{s}\times\Re
such that

\displaystyle u^{\top}N_{j,g}=cc_{1,j},\penalty\ j\in[J],

or equivalently,

\displaystyle\left(I_{J}+(-a_{1}\xi_{1},\cdots,-a_{J}\xi_{J})^{\top}\iota_{J}^{\top}\right)(g_{1}\mathcal{Z}_{1},\cdots,g_{J}\mathcal{Z}_{j})^{\top}\tilde{Q}^{\top}\lambda_{\beta}u=c(c_{1,g},\cdots,c_{J,g})^{\top}.

This occurs with probability zero if
J-1>d_{r}
as
\{g_{j}\mathcal{Z}_{j}\}_{j\in[J]}
are independent and non-degenerate normal vectors and the matrix
\left(I_{J}+(-a_{1}\xi_{1},\cdots,-a_{J}\xi_{J})^{\top}\iota_{J}^{\top}\right)
has rank
J-1
. Therefore, the matrix
\mathbb{M}\equiv M_{1}-\frac{M_{2}M_{2}^{\top}}{\overline{c}^{2}}
is invertible with probability one. Specifically, denote
\mathbb{M}
as
\mathbb{M}(g)
to highlight its dependence on
g
.
We have
\max_{g\in\textbf{G}_{s}}(\lambda_{\min}(\mathbb{M}(g)))^{-1}=O_{P}(1)
.
In addition, denote
\frac{M_{2}}{\overline{c}}+\overline{c}\mu
as
\mathbb{V}
, which is a
d_{r}\times 1
vector. Then, we have

\displaystyle\left[\sum_{j\in[J]}(N_{j,g}+c_{j,g}\mu)(N_{j,g}+c_{j,g}\mu)^{\top}\right]^{-1}=[\mathbb{M}+\mathbb{V}\mathbb{V}^{\top}]^{-1}=\mathbb{M}^{-1}-\mathbb{M}^{-1}\mathbb{V}(1+\mathbb{V}^{\top}\mathbb{M}^{-1}\mathbb{V})^{-1}\mathbb{V}^{\top}\mathbb{M}^{-1},

where the second equality is due to the Sherman–Morrison–Woodbury formula.

Next, we note that

\displaystyle N_{0,g}+c_{0,g}\mu=N_{0,g}+c_{0,g}\left(\frac{\mathbb{V}}{\overline{c}}-\frac{M_{2}}{\overline{c}^{2}}\right)\equiv\mathbb{M}_{0}+\frac{c_{0,g}}{\overline{c}}\mathbb{V},

where

\mathbb{M}_{0}=N_{0,g}-\frac{c_{0,g}M_{2}}{\overline{c}^{2}}=N_{0,g}-\frac{c_{0,g}(\sum_{j\in[J]}c_{j,g}N_{j,g})}{\sum_{j\in[J]}c_{j,g}^{2}}.

With these notations, we have

\displaystyle(N_{0,g}+c_{0,g}\mu)^{\top}\left[\sum_{j\in[J]}(N_{j,g}+c_{j,g}\mu)(N_{j,g}+c_{j,g}\mu)^{\top}\right]^{-1}(N_{0,g}+c_{0,g}\mu)

\displaystyle=\left(\mathbb{M}_{0}+\frac{c_{0,g}}{\overline{c}}\mathbb{V}\right)^{\top}(\mathbb{M}^{-1}-\mathbb{M}^{-1}\mathbb{V}(1+\mathbb{V}^{\top}\mathbb{M}^{-1}\mathbb{V})^{-1}\mathbb{V}^{\top}\mathbb{M}^{-1})\left(\mathbb{M}_{0}+\frac{c_{0,g}}{\overline{c}}\mathbb{V}\right)

\displaystyle\leq 2\mathbb{M}_{0}^{\top}(\mathbb{M}^{-1}-\mathbb{M}^{-1}\mathbb{V}(1+\mathbb{V}^{\top}\mathbb{M}^{-1}\mathbb{V})^{-1}\mathbb{V}^{\top}\mathbb{M}^{-1})\mathbb{M}_{0}

\displaystyle+\frac{2c_{0,g}^{2}}{\overline{c}^{2}}\mathbb{V}^{\top}(\mathbb{M}^{-1}-\mathbb{M}^{-1}\mathbb{V}(1+\mathbb{V}^{\top}\mathbb{M}^{-1}\mathbb{V})^{-1}\mathbb{V}^{\top}\mathbb{M}^{-1})\mathbb{V}

\displaystyle\leq 2\mathbb{M}_{0}^{\top}\mathbb{M}^{-1}\mathbb{M}_{0}+\frac{2c_{0,g}^{2}}{\overline{c}^{2}}\frac{\mathbb{V}^{\top}\mathbb{M}^{-1}\mathbb{V}}{1+\mathbb{V}^{\top}\mathbb{M}^{-1}\mathbb{V}}\leq 2\mathbb{M}_{0}^{\top}\mathbb{M}^{-1}\mathbb{M}_{0}+\frac{2c_{0,g}^{2}}{\overline{c}^{2}}

\displaystyle\leq 2(\lambda_{\min}(\mathbb{M}))^{-1}\left\|N_{0,g}-\frac{c_{0,g}(\sum_{j\in[J]}c_{j,q}N_{j,g})}{\sum_{j\in[J]}c_{j,g}^{2}}\right\|^{2}+\frac{2c_{0,g}^{2}}{\sum_{j\in[J]}c_{j,g}^{2}}\equiv C(g),

where the first inequality holds because
(u+v)^{\top}A(u+v)\leq 2(u^{\top}Au+v^{\top}Av)
for some
d_{r}\times d_{r}
positive semidefinite matrix
A
and
u,v\in\Re^{d_{r}}
, the second inequality holds due to the fact that
\mathbb{M}^{-1}\mathbb{V}(1+\mathbb{V}^{\top}\mathbb{M}^{-1}\mathbb{V})^{-1}\mathbb{V}^{\top}\mathbb{M}^{-1}
is positive semidefinite, the third inequality holds because
\mathbb{V}^{\top}\mathbb{M}^{-1}\mathbb{V}
is a nonnegative scalar, and the last holds by substituting in the expressions for
\mathbb{M}_{0}
and
\overline{c}
.

Then, we have

\displaystyle\max_{g\in\textbf{G}_{s}}T_{CR,\infty}(g)\leq\max_{g\in\textbf{G}_{s}}C(g).

(62)

Combining (
61
) and (
62
), we have, as
||\mu||_{2}\rightarrow\infty
,

\displaystyle\liminf_{n\rightarrow\infty}\mathbb{P}\{T_{CR,n}>\hat{c}_{CR,n}(1-\alpha)\}\geq\mathbb{P}\left\{T_{CR,\infty}(\iota_{J})>(T_{CR,\infty})^{(k^{*})}\right\}\geq\mathbb{P}\left\{T_{CR,\infty}(\iota_{J})>\max_{g\in\textbf{G}_{s}}T_{CR,\infty}(g)\right\}

\displaystyle=1-\mathbb{P}\left\{T_{CR,\infty}(\iota_{J})\leq\max_{g\in\textbf{G}_{s}}T_{CR,\infty}(g)\right\}\geq 1-\mathbb{P}\left\{c||\mu||^{2}_{2}-M\leq\max_{g\in\textbf{G}_{s}}C(g)\right\}-\delta\rightarrow 1-\delta,

where the second inequality is by the fact that
k^{*}\leq|\textbf{G}_{s}|
, and thus,
(T_{CR,\infty})^{(k^{*})}\leq\max_{g\in\textbf{G}_{s}}T_{CR,\infty}(g)
and the last convergence holds because
\max_{g\in\textbf{G}_{s}}C(g)=O_{P}(1)
and does not depend on
\mu
. As
\delta
is arbitrary, we can let
\delta\rightarrow 0
and obtain the desired result.

For the proof of part (ii), let
\tilde{c}_{CR,n}(1-\alpha)
denote the
(1-\alpha)
quantile
of

\displaystyle\left\{|(\lambda_{\beta}^{\top}\hat{\beta}^{*}_{tsls,g}-\lambda_{0})|/\sqrt{\lambda_{\beta}^{\top}\widehat{V}\lambda_{\beta}}:g\in\textbf{G}\right\},

i.e., the bootstrap statistic
T_{n}^{*}(g)
studentized by the original CRVE instead of the bootstrap CRVE.
Because
d_{r}=1
, we have

\displaystyle 1\{T_{n}>\hat{c}_{n}(1-\alpha)\}=1\{T_{CR,n}>\tilde{c}_{CR,n}(1-\alpha)\}.

Therefore, we have

\displaystyle\liminf_{n\rightarrow\infty}\mathbb{P}(\phi^{cr}_{n}\geq\phi_{n})

\displaystyle=\liminf_{n\rightarrow\infty}\mathbb{P}(1\{T_{CR,n}>\hat{c}_{CR,n}(1-\alpha)\}\geq 1\{T_{CR,n}>\tilde{c}_{CR,n}(1-\alpha)\})

\displaystyle=1-\limsup_{n\rightarrow\infty}\mathbb{P}(1\{T_{CR,n}>\hat{c}_{CR,n}(1-\alpha)\}<1\{T_{CR,n}>\tilde{c}_{CR,n}(1-\alpha)\})

\displaystyle\geq 1-\limsup_{n\rightarrow\infty}\mathbb{P}(\sqrt{n}\hat{c}_{CR,n}(1-\alpha)>\sqrt{n}\tilde{c}_{CR,n}(1-\alpha))

\displaystyle\geq 1-\limsup_{n\rightarrow\infty}\mathbb{P}\left(\left(\frac{r_{n}^{2}}{n}\lambda_{\beta}^{\top}\widehat{V}\lambda_{\beta}\right)^{1/2}\sqrt{n}\hat{c}_{CR,n}(1-\alpha)+\max_{g\in\textbf{G}}|r_{n}\lambda_{\beta}^{\top}(\hat{\beta}^{*}_{tsls,g}-\beta_{n})|>|\mu|\right),

where we use the fact that

\displaystyle\sqrt{n}\tilde{c}_{CR,n}(1-\alpha)\geq\left(\frac{r_{n}^{2}}{n}\lambda_{\beta}^{\top}\widehat{V}\lambda_{\beta}\right)^{-1/2}\left[|\mu|-\max_{g\in\textbf{G}}|r_{n}\lambda_{\beta}^{\top}(\hat{\beta}^{*}_{tsls,g}-\beta_{n})|\right].

Further note that
\max_{g\in\textbf{G}}|r_{n}\lambda_{\beta}^{\top}(\hat{\beta}^{*}_{tsls,g}-\beta_{n})|=O_{P}(1)
and does not depend on
\mu
, and

\displaystyle\left(\frac{r_{n}^{2}}{n}\lambda_{\beta}^{\top}\widehat{V}\lambda_{\beta}\right)^{-1}\xrightarrow{\hskip 4.46255ptd\hskip 4.46255pt}A_{r,CR,\iota_{J}}

and
A_{r,CR,\iota_{J}}
does not depend on
\mu
either.

Last, although
\hat{c}_{CR,n}(1-\alpha)
depends on
\mu
, we have

\displaystyle\sqrt{n}\hat{c}_{CR,n}(1-\alpha)\leq\sqrt{n}\max_{g\in\textbf{G}_{s}}T_{CR,n}^{*}(g)\xrightarrow{\hskip 4.46255ptd\hskip 4.46255pt}\max_{g\in\textbf{G}_{s}}T_{CR,\infty}(g)\leq\max_{g\in\textbf{G}_{s}}C(g)=O_{P}(1)

for some
C(g)
that does not depend on
\mu
as has been proved in Part (i).
Therefore, for any
\delta>0
, there exists a constant
c_{\mu}>0
, such that when
|\mu|>c_{\mu}
,

\displaystyle\limsup_{n\rightarrow\infty}\mathbb{P}\left(\left(\frac{r_{n}^{2}}{n}\lambda_{\beta}^{\top}\widehat{V}\lambda_{\beta}\right)^{1/2}\sqrt{n}\hat{c}_{CR,n}(1-\alpha)+\max_{g\in\textbf{G}}|r_{n}\lambda_{\beta}^{\top}(\hat{\beta}^{*}_{tsls,g}-\beta_{n})|>|\mu|\right)

\displaystyle\leq\limsup_{n\rightarrow\infty}\mathbb{P}\left(\left(\frac{r_{n}^{2}}{n}\lambda_{\beta}^{\top}\widehat{V}\lambda_{\beta}\right)^{1/2}\sqrt{n}\max_{g\in\textbf{G}_{s}}T_{CR,n}(g)+C>|\mu|\right)+\delta/2

\displaystyle\leq\mathbb{P}\left(A_{r,CR,\iota_{J}}^{-1/2}\max_{g\in\textbf{G}_{s}}T_{CR,\infty}(g)+C\geq|\mu|\right)+\delta/2

\displaystyle\leq\mathbb{P}\left(A_{r,CR,\iota_{J}}^{-1/2}\max_{g\in\textbf{G}_{s}}C(g)+C\geq c_{\mu}\right)+\delta/2\leq\delta,

where
C
in the first inequality is a constant such that for
n
being sufficiently large,

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\left(\max_{g\in\textbf{G}}|r_{n}\lambda_{\beta}^{\top}(\hat{\beta}^{*}_{tsls,g}-\beta_{n})|\geq C\right)\leq\delta/2,

the second inequality is by Portmanteau theorem, and the last inequality holds if
c_{\mu}
is sufficiently large. This concludes the proof.
\blacksquare

17
Proof of Theorem
6.1

The proof for the
AR_{n}
-based wild bootstrap test follows similar arguments as those in
Theorem
4.1
,
and thus we keep exposition more concise.
Let
\mathbb{S}\equiv\otimes_{j\in[J]}\textbf{R}^{d_{z}}\times\textbf{R}^{d_{z}\times d_{z}}
and write an element
s\in\mathbb{S}

by
s=(\{s_{1j}:j\in[J]\},s_{2})
where
s_{1j}\in\textbf{R}^{d_{z}}
for any
j\in[J]
.
Define the function
T_{AR}
:
\mathbb{S}\rightarrow\textbf{R}
to be given by

\displaystyle T_{AR}(s)=\bigg\|\sum_{j\in[J]}s_{1j}\bigg\|_{s_{2}}.

(63)

Given this notation we can define the statistics
S_{n},\widehat{S}_{n}\in\mathbb{S}
as

\displaystyle S_{n}=\left\{\frac{r_{n}}{n}\sum_{i\in I_{n,j}}\widetilde{Z}_{i,j}\varepsilon_{i,j}:j\in[J],\hat{A}_{z}\right\},\hskip 11.74988pt\widehat{S}_{n}=\left\{\frac{r_{n}}{n}\sum_{i\in I_{n,j}}\widetilde{Z}_{i,j}\bar{\varepsilon}^{r}_{i,j}:j\in[J],\hat{A}_{z}\right\},

where
\bar{\varepsilon}^{r}_{i,j}=y_{i,j}-X_{i,j}^{\top}\beta_{0}-W^{\top}_{i,j}\bar{\gamma}^{r}
.
Note that by the Frisch-Waugh-Lovell theorem,

\displaystyle r_{n}AR_{n}

\displaystyle=\bigg\|\sum_{j\in[J]}\frac{r_{n}}{n}\sum_{i\in I_{n,j}}\widetilde{Z}_{i,j}\varepsilon_{i,j}\bigg\|_{\hat{A}_{z}}=T_{AR}(S_{n}).

(64)

Therefore, letting
k^{*}\equiv\lceil|\textbf{G}|(1-\alpha)\rceil
, we obtain from (
64
)-(
69
) that

\displaystyle 1\left\{AR_{n}>\hat{c}_{AR,n}(1-\alpha)\right\}=1\left\{T_{AR}(S_{n})>T^{(k^{*})}_{AR}(\widehat{S}_{n}|\textbf{G})\right\}.

Furthermore, note that we have

\displaystyle T_{AR}\left(-\iota_{J}\widehat{S}_{n}\right)

\displaystyle=T_{AR}\left(\iota_{J}\widehat{S}_{n}\right)=\bigg\|\sum_{j\in[J]}\frac{r_{n}}{n}\sum_{i\in I_{n,j}}\widetilde{Z}_{i,j}\left(y_{i,j}-X_{i,j}^{\top}\beta_{0}-W_{i,j}^{\top}\bar{\gamma}^{r}\right)\bigg\|_{\hat{A}_{z}}

\displaystyle=\bigg\|\sum_{j\in[J]}\frac{r_{n}}{n}\sum_{i\in I_{n,j}}\widetilde{Z}_{i,j}\left(\varepsilon_{i,j}-W_{i,j}^{\top}(\bar{\gamma}^{r}-\gamma)\right)\bigg\|_{\hat{A}_{z}}=T_{AR}\left(S_{n}\right),

(65)

where the third equality follows from
\sum_{j\in[J]}\sum_{i\in I_{n,j}}\widetilde{Z}_{i,j}W_{i,j}^{\top}=0
.
(
17
) implies that if
k^{*}>|\textbf{G}|-2
,
then
1\{T_{AR}(S_{n})>T_{AR}^{(k^{*})}(\widehat{S}_{n}|\textbf{G})\}=0
,
and this gives the upper bound in Theorem
6.1
.
We therefore assume that
k^{*}\leq|\textbf{G}|-2
, in which case

\displaystyle\limsup_{n\rightarrow\infty}\mathbb{P}\{T_{AR}(S_{n})>T^{(k^{*})}_{AR}(\widehat{S}_{n}|\textbf{G})\}

\displaystyle=\limsup_{n\rightarrow\infty}\mathbb{P}\{T_{AR}(S_{n})>T^{(k^{*})}_{AR}(\widehat{S}_{n}|\textbf{G}\setminus\{\pm\iota_{J}\})\}

\displaystyle\leq\limsup_{n\rightarrow\infty}\mathbb{P}\{T_{AR}(S_{n})\geq T^{(k^{*})}_{AR}(\widehat{S}_{n}|\textbf{G}\setminus\{\pm\iota_{J}\})\}.

(66)

Then, to examine the right hand side of (
17
),
first note that by Assumptions
1
and
5
,
and the continuous mapping theorem we have

\displaystyle\left\{\frac{r_{n}}{n}\sum_{i\in I_{n,j}}\widetilde{Z}_{i,j}\varepsilon_{i,j}:j\in[J],\hat{A}_{z}\right\}\xrightarrow{\hskip 4.46255ptd\hskip 4.46255pt}\left\{\mathcal{Z}_{j}:j\in[J],A_{z}\right\}\equiv S,

(67)

where
\xi_{j}>0
for all
j\in[J]
. Furthermore, by Assumptions
1
(i), Lemma
12.3
, and
\beta_{n}=\beta_{0}
, we have

\displaystyle\frac{r_{n}}{n}\sum_{i\in I_{n,j}}\widetilde{Z}_{i,j}\bar{\varepsilon}_{i,j}^{r}=\frac{r_{n}}{n}\sum_{i\in I_{n,j}}\widetilde{Z}_{i,j}\varepsilon_{i,j}-\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i\in I_{n,j}}\widetilde{Z}_{i,j}W_{i,j}^{\top}r_{n}(\bar{\gamma}^{r}-\gamma)=\frac{r_{n}}{n}\sum_{i\in I_{n,j}}\widetilde{Z}_{i,j}\varepsilon_{i,j}+o_{P}(1),

and thus, for every
g\in\textbf{G}
,

\displaystyle T_{AR}(g\widehat{S}_{n})=T_{AR}(gS_{n})+o_{P}(1).

(68)

We thus obtain from results in (
67
)-(
68
) and the continuous mapping theorem that

\displaystyle\left(T_{AR}(S_{n}),\left\{T_{AR}(g\widehat{S}_{n}):g\in\textbf{G}\right\}\right)\xrightarrow{\hskip 4.46255ptd\hskip 4.46255pt}\left(T_{AR}(S),\{T_{AR}(gS):g\in\textbf{G}\}\right).

Then, by the Portmanteau’s theorem and the properties of randomization tests, we have

\displaystyle\limsup_{n\rightarrow\infty}\mathbb{P}\left\{AR_{n}>\hat{c}_{AR,n}(1-\alpha)\right\}

\displaystyle\leq\mathbb{P}\left\{T_{AR}(S)\geq T_{AR}^{k^{*}}(\text{G}\setminus\{\pm\iota_{J}\})\right\}=\mathbb{P}\left\{T_{AR}(S)>T_{AR}^{k^{*}}(\text{G})\right\}\leq\alpha.

The lower bounds follow by applying similar arguments as those for
Theorem
4.1
.

For the
AR_{CR,n}
-based wild bootstrap test, define the statistics
S_{CR,n},\widehat{S}_{CR,n}\in\mathbb{S}
as

\displaystyle S_{CR,n}=\left\{\frac{r_{n}}{n}\sum_{i\in I_{n,j}}\widetilde{Z}_{i,j}\varepsilon_{i,j}:j\in[J],\frac{r_{n}^{2}}{n}\hat{A}_{CR}\right\},\widehat{S}_{CR,n}=\left\{\frac{r_{n}}{n}\sum_{i\in I_{n,j}}\widetilde{Z}_{i,j}\bar{\varepsilon}^{r}_{i,j}:j\in[J],\frac{r_{n}^{2}}{n}\hat{A}_{CR}\right\}.

Then, we have for any action
g\in\textbf{G}
that

\displaystyle\sqrt{n}AR^{*}_{n}(g)

\displaystyle=\bigg\|\sum_{j\in[J]}\frac{r_{n}}{n}\sum_{i\in I_{n,j}}g_{j}\widetilde{Z}_{i,j}\bar{\varepsilon}^{r}_{i,j}\bigg\|_{\frac{r_{n}^{2}}{n}\hat{A}_{CR}}=T_{AR}(g\widehat{S}_{n}).

(69)

We set
E_{n}\in\textbf{R}
to equal

E_{n}\equiv 1\left\{\frac{r_{n}^{2}}{n^{2}}\sum_{j\in J}\sum_{i\in I_{n,j}}\sum_{k\in I_{n,j}}\widetilde{Z}_{i,j}\widetilde{Z}_{k,j}^{\top}\bar{\varepsilon}^{r}_{i,j}\bar{\varepsilon}^{r}_{k,j}\;\text{is invertible}\right\},

and have

\displaystyle\liminf_{n\rightarrow\infty}\mathbb{P}\{E_{n}=1\}=1.

(70)

In addition,
similar to the case with
AR_{n}
and
AR^{*}_{n}(g)
, we have under
\beta_{n}=\beta_{0}
,

\displaystyle T_{AR}\left(g\widehat{S}_{CR,n}\right)=T_{AR}\left(gS_{CR,n}\right)+o_{P}(1)\;\;\text{for every $g\in\textbf{G}$},

\displaystyle S_{CR,n}\xrightarrow{\hskip 4.46255ptd\hskip 4.46255pt}\left\{\mathcal{Z}_{j}:j\in[J],A_{CR}\right\}\equiv S_{CR},

where
A_{CR}=\sum_{j\in[J]}\mathcal{Z}_{j}\mathcal{Z}_{j}^{\top}
,
and

\displaystyle\left(T_{AR}(S_{CR,n}),\{T_{AR}(g\widehat{S}_{CR,n}):g\in\textbf{G}\}\right)\xrightarrow{\hskip 4.46255ptd\hskip 4.46255pt}\left(T_{AR}(S_{CR}),\{T_{AR}(gS_{CR}):g\in\textbf{G}\}\right).

(71)

Therefore, we have

\displaystyle\limsup_{n\rightarrow\infty}\mathbb{P}\left\{AR_{CR,n}>\hat{c}_{AR,CR,n}(1-\alpha)\right\}

\displaystyle\leq\limsup_{n\rightarrow\infty}\mathbb{P}\left\{AR_{CR,n}\geq\hat{c}_{AR,CR,n}(1-\alpha);E_{n}=1\right\}

\displaystyle\leq\mathbb{P}\left\{T_{AR}(S_{CR})\geq T_{AR}^{(k^{*})}(S_{CR}|\textbf{G})\right\},

which follows from (
70
), (
71
), the continuous mapping theorem
and Portmanteau’s theorem.
The claim of the upper bound in the theorem then follows from similar arguments as those in Theorem
4.1
.

\blacksquare

18
Proof of Theorem
6.2

Define
AR_{\infty}(g)=||\sum_{j\in[J]}g_{j}\mathcal{Z}_{j}+\sum_{j\in[J]}\xi_{j}g_{j}a_{j}Q_{\widetilde{Z}X}\mu||_{A_{z}}
.
In particular, notice that
AR_{\infty}(\iota_{J})=||\sum_{j\in[J]}\mathcal{Z}_{j}+Q_{\widetilde{Z}X}\mu||_{A_{z}}
since
\sum_{j\in[J]}\xi_{j}a_{j}=1
.
Following same arguments in the proof of Theorem
6.1
, we can show that, under
\mathcal{H}_{1,n}
with
\lambda_{\beta}=I_{d_{x}}
,

\displaystyle(r_{n}AR_{n},\{r_{n}AR_{n}^{*}(g)\}_{g\in\textbf{G}})\xrightarrow{\hskip 4.46255ptd\hskip 4.46255pt}(AR_{\infty}(\iota_{J}),\{AR_{\infty}(g)\}_{g\in\textbf{G}}).

Similar to the proofs of Theorems
4.2
and
5.2
, in order to establish Theorem
6.2
, it suffices to show that as
||Q_{\widetilde{Z}X}\mu||_{2}\rightarrow\infty
,

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\{AR_{\infty}(\iota_{J})>\max_{g\in\textbf{G}_{s}}AR_{\infty}(g)\}\rightarrow 1.

By Triangular inequality, we have

AR_{\infty}(\iota_{J})\geq||Q_{\widetilde{Z}X}\mu||_{A_{z}}-O_{P}(1)
,
and

\max_{g\in\textbf{G}_{s}}AR_{\infty}(g)\leq\max_{g\in\textbf{G}_{s}}|\sum_{j\in[J]}g_{j}\xi_{j}a_{j}|||Q_{\widetilde{Z}X}\mu||_{A_{z}}+O_{P}(1).

In addition,
\max_{g\in\textbf{G}_{s}}|\sum_{j\in[J]}g_{j}\xi_{j}a_{j}|<1
so that as
||Q_{\widetilde{Z}X}\mu||_{2}\rightarrow\infty
,
||Q_{\widetilde{Z}X}\mu||_{A_{z}}\rightarrow\infty
and

\displaystyle||Q_{\widetilde{Z}X}\mu||_{A_{z}}-\max_{g\in\textbf{G}_{s}}|\sum_{j\in[J]}g_{j}\xi_{j}a_{j}|||Q_{\widetilde{Z}X}\mu||_{A_{z}}\rightarrow\infty.

This concludes the proof.
\blacksquare

19
Further Results for the Weak-IV-Robust Statistics

First, we show that in the specific case with one endogenous regressor and one IV, if the wild bootstrap procedure for the
AR_{n}
statistic is applied to the unstudentized Wald statistic
T_{n}
, then the resulting test will be asymptotically equivalent to the
AR_{n}
-based bootstrap test, both under the null and the alternative.
More precisely, for
T_{n}=|\hat{\beta}-\beta_{0}|
, where
\hat{\beta}
is the TSLS estimator, the wild bootstrap generates

T^{s*}_{n}(g)=\left|\widehat{Q}_{\widetilde{Z}X}^{-1}\frac{1}{n}\sum_{j\in[J]}\sum_{i\in I_{n,j}}g_{j}\widetilde{Z}_{i,j}\hat{\varepsilon}^{r}_{i,j}\right|.

(72)

Notice that in this case, the restricted TSLS estimator
\hat{\gamma}^{r}
and the restricted OLS estimator
\bar{\gamma}^{r}
are the same, which implies the residuals
\hat{\varepsilon}^{r}_{i,j}
and
\bar{\varepsilon}^{r}_{i,j}
defined in Sections
4
and
6
, respectively, are the same.
Let
\hat{c}^{s}_{n}(1-\alpha)
denote the
(1-\alpha)
-th quantile of

\{T^{s*}_{n}(g)\}_{g\in\textbf{G}}
.
We show this equivalence below.

Theorem 19.1
.

Suppose that
d_{x}=d_{z}=1
,
\liminf_{n\rightarrow\infty}\mathbb{P}(\widehat{Q}_{\widetilde{Z}X}\neq 0)=1
,
\hat{\beta}
in the Wald test is computed via TSLS, and Assumption
1
(iv) holds. Then,

\displaystyle\liminf_{n\rightarrow\infty}\mathbb{P}\{\phi_{n}^{s}=\phi_{n}^{ar}\}=1,

where
\phi_{n}^{s}=1\{T_{n}>\hat{c}^{s}_{n}(1-\alpha)\}
and
\phi_{n}^{ar}=1\{AR_{n}>\hat{c}^{ar}_{n}(1-\alpha)\}
.

Several remarks are in order.
First, with one endogenous variable and one IV, the Jacobian matrix
\widehat{Q}_{\widetilde{Z}X}
is just a scalar, which shows up in both the Wald statistic
T_{n}
and the bootstrap critical value. After the cancellation of
\widehat{Q}_{\widetilde{Z}X}
,

T_{n}
and its critical value are numerically the same as their AR counterparts, which leads to Theorem
19.1
.
Second, for
\widehat{Q}_{\widetilde{Z}X}
to be cancelled, we only need
\liminf_{n\rightarrow\infty}\mathbb{P}\{\widehat{Q}_{\widetilde{Z}X}\neq 0\}=1
, which is very mild. It holds when
\widehat{Q}_{\widetilde{Z}X}
is continuously distributed.
Even when both
\widetilde{Z}_{i,j}
and
X_{i,j}
are discrete, it still holds if there exists at least one strong IV cluster, i.e.,
Q_{\widetilde{Z}X}\neq 0
, where
Q_{\widetilde{Z}X}
is the probability limit of
\widehat{Q}_{\widetilde{Z}X}
.
When both
\widetilde{Z}_{i,j}
and
X_{i,j}
are discrete and
Q_{\widetilde{Z}X}=0
, this condition still holds if some type of CLT holds such that
r_{n}\widehat{Q}_{\widetilde{Z}X}\xrightarrow{\hskip 4.46255ptd\hskip 4.46255pt}N(c,\sigma^{2})
, as
N(c,\sigma^{2})
is continuous. Third, the robustness of the
T_{n}
-based bootstrap test in (
72
) does not carry over to the general case with multiple IVs as
T_{n}
and its bootstrap statistic can no longer be reduced to their AR counterparts.
Fourth, the robustness to weak IV cannot be extended to the
T_{CR,n}
-based bootstrap test, for which we have to further bootstrap the CRVE.

Second, we discuss wild bootstrap inference with other weak-IV-robust statistics.
To introduce the test statistics,
we define the sample Jacobian as

\displaystyle\widehat{G}=\left(\widehat{G}_{1},...,\widehat{G}_{d_{x}}\right)\in\textbf{R}^{d_{z}\times d_{x}},\hskip 11.74988pt\widehat{G}_{l}

\displaystyle=n^{-1}\sum_{j\in[J]}\sum_{i\in I_{n,j}}\widetilde{Z}_{i,j}X_{i,j,l},\;\text{for}\;l=1,...,d_{x},

and define the orthogonalized sample Jacobian as

\displaystyle\widehat{D}

\displaystyle=\left(\widehat{D}_{1},...,\widehat{D}_{d_{x}}\right)\in\textbf{R}^{d_{z}\times d_{x}},\;\hskip 11.74988pt\widehat{D}_{l}=\widehat{G}_{l}-\widehat{\Gamma}_{l}\widehat{\Omega}^{-1}\widehat{f}\in\textbf{R}^{d_{z}},\;

where

\widehat{\Omega}=n^{-1}\sum_{j\in[J]}\sum_{i\in I_{n,j}}\sum_{k\in I_{n,j}}f_{i,j}f_{k,j}^{\top}
,
and
\widehat{\Gamma}_{l}=n^{-1}\sum_{j\in[J]}\sum_{i\in I_{n,j}}\sum_{k\in I_{n,j}}\left(\widetilde{Z}_{i,j}X_{i,j,l}\right)f_{k,j}^{\top},

for
l=1,...,d_{x}
.
Therefore, under the null
\beta_{n}=\beta_{0}

and the framework where the number of clusters tends to infinity,

\widehat{D}

equals the sample Jacobian matrix
\widehat{G}
adjusted to be asymptotically independent of
\widehat{f}
.

Then,
the cluster-robust version of
Kleibergen 2002
;
Kleibergen 2005
’s LM statistic is defined as

\displaystyle LM_{n}

\displaystyle=n\widehat{f}^{\top}\widehat{\Omega}^{-1/2}P_{\widehat{\Omega}^{-1/2}\widehat{D}}\widehat{\Omega}^{-1/2}\widehat{f}.

In addition,
the conditional quasi-likelihood ratio (CQLR) statistic in
Kleibergen 2005
,
Newey and
Windmeijer 2009
,
and
Guggenberger
et al. 2012
are adapted from
Moreira 2003
’s
conditional likelihood ratio (CLR) test,
and its cluster-robust version takes the form

\displaystyle LR_{n}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\left(AR_{CR,n}-rk_{n}+\sqrt{\left(AR_{CR,n}-rk_{n}\right)^{2}+4LM_{n}\cdot rk_{n}}\right),

where
rk_{n}
is a conditioning statistic and we let
rk_{n}=n\widehat{D}^{\top}\widehat{\Omega}^{-1}\widehat{D}
.
36
36

36

This choice follows
Newey and
Windmeijer 2009
.

Kleibergen 2005
uses alternative formula for
rk_{n}
, and

Andrews and
Guggenberger 2019
introduce alternative CQLR test statistic.

The wild bootstrap procedure for the LM and CQLR tests is as follows.
We compute

\displaystyle\widehat{D}^{*}_{g}

\displaystyle=\left(\widehat{D}^{*}_{1,g},...,\widehat{D}^{*}_{d_{x},g}\right),\hskip 11.74988pt\widehat{D}^{*}_{l,g}=\widehat{G}_{l}-\widehat{\Gamma}^{*}_{l,g}\widehat{\Omega}^{-1}\widehat{f}^{*}_{g},\;

\displaystyle\widehat{\Gamma}^{*}_{l,g}

\displaystyle=n^{-1}\sum_{j\in[J]}\sum_{i\in I_{n,j}}\sum_{k\in I_{n,j}}\left(\widetilde{Z}_{i,j}X_{i,j,l}\right)f^{*}_{k,j}(g_{j})^{\top},\;l=1,...,d_{x},

for any
g=(g_{1},...,g_{q})\in\textbf{G}
,
where the definition of
\widehat{f}_{g}^{*}
and
f^{*}_{k,j}(g_{j})
is the same as that in Section
6
.
Then, we compute the bootstrap analogues of the test statistics as

\displaystyle LM^{*}_{n}(g)

\displaystyle=n(\widehat{f}^{*}_{g})^{\top}\widehat{\Omega}^{-1/2}P_{\widehat{\Omega}^{-1/2}\widehat{D}^{*}_{g}}\widehat{\Omega}^{-1/2}\widehat{f}^{*}_{g},

\displaystyle LR^{*}_{n}(g)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\left(AR^{*}_{CR,n}(g)-rk_{n}+\sqrt{\left(AR^{*}_{CR,n}(g)-rk_{n}\right)^{2}+4LM^{*}_{n}(g)\cdot rk_{n}}\right).

Let
\hat{c}_{LM,n}(1-\alpha)
and
\hat{c}_{LR,n}(1-\alpha)

denote the
(1-\alpha)
-th quantile of
\{LM_{n}^{*}(g)\}_{g\in\textbf{G}}
and
\{LR^{*}_{n}(g)\}_{g\in\textbf{G}}
, respectively.
We notice that with at least one strong cluster,

\displaystyle LM_{n}\xrightarrow{\hskip 4.46255ptd\hskip 4.46255pt}\bigg\|\left(\widetilde{D}^{\top}\left(\sum_{j\in[J]}\xi_{j}\mathcal{Z}_{\varepsilon,j}\mathcal{Z}_{\varepsilon,j}^{\top}\right)^{-1}\widetilde{D}\right)^{-1/2}\widetilde{D}^{\top}\left(\sum_{j\in[J]}\xi_{j}\mathcal{Z}_{\varepsilon,j}\mathcal{Z}_{\varepsilon,j}^{\top}\right)^{-1}\sum_{j\in[J]}\sqrt{\xi_{j}}\mathcal{Z}_{\varepsilon,j}\bigg\|^{2},

where
\widetilde{D}=\left(\widetilde{D}_{1},...,\widetilde{D}_{d_{x}}\right)
, and for

l=1,...,d_{x}
,

\widetilde{D}_{l}=Q_{\widetilde{Z}X}-\left\{\sum_{j\in[J]}\left(\xi_{j}Q_{\widetilde{Z}X,j,l}\right)\left(\sqrt{\xi_{j}}\mathcal{Z}_{\varepsilon,j}\right)\right\}\left\{\sum_{j\in[J]}\xi_{j}\mathcal{Z}_{\varepsilon,j}\mathcal{Z}_{\varepsilon,j}^{\top}\right\}^{-1}\sum_{j\in[J]}\sqrt{\xi_{j}}\mathcal{Z}_{\varepsilon,j}.

Although the limiting distribution is nonstandard,
we are able to establish the validity results by connecting the bootstrap LM test with the randomization test and by showing the asymptotic equivalence of the bootstrap LM and CQLR tests in this case.
We conjecture that similar results can also be established for other weak-IV-robust statistics proposed in the literature.

Theorem 19.2
.

Suppose Assumptions
1
,
2
(i), and
3
hold,
\beta_{n}=\beta_{0}
, and
q>d_{z}
, then

\displaystyle\alpha-\frac{1}{2^{J-1}}\leq\liminf_{n\rightarrow\infty}\mathbb{P}\{LM_{n}>\hat{c}_{LM,n}(1-\alpha)\}\leq\limsup_{n\rightarrow\infty}\mathbb{P}\{LM_{n}>\hat{c}_{LM,n}(1-\alpha)\}\leq\alpha+\frac{1}{2^{J-1}};

\displaystyle\alpha-\frac{1}{2^{J-1}}\leq\liminf_{n\rightarrow\infty}\mathbb{P}\{LR_{n}>\hat{c}_{LR,n}(1-\alpha)\}\leq\limsup_{n\rightarrow\infty}\mathbb{P}\{LR_{n}>\hat{c}_{LR,n}(1-\alpha)\}\leq\alpha+\frac{1}{2^{J-1}}.

20
Proof of Theorem
19.1

Notice that when
d_{x}=d_{z}=1
,
\widehat{Q}_{\widetilde{Z}X}
is a scalar,
and the restricted TSLS estimator
\hat{\gamma}^{r}
is equivalent to the restricted OLS estimator
\bar{\gamma}^{r}
, which is well defined by Assumption
1
(iv),
so that
\hat{\varepsilon}_{i,j}^{r}=\bar{\varepsilon}_{i,j}^{r}
.
Therefore,

\displaystyle AR_{n}=\left|\frac{1}{n}\sum_{j\in[J]}\sum_{i\in I_{n,j}}\widetilde{Z}_{i,j}\bar{\varepsilon}^{r}_{i,j}\right|=\left|\frac{1}{n}\sum_{j\in[J]}\sum_{i\in I_{n,j}}\widetilde{Z}_{i,j}\hat{\varepsilon}^{r}_{i,j}\right|,

and whenever
\widehat{Q}_{\widetilde{Z}X}\neq 0
,

\displaystyle T_{n}

\displaystyle=|(\hat{\beta}_{tsls}-\beta_{n})+(\beta_{n}-\beta_{0})|

\displaystyle=\left|\widehat{Q}_{\widetilde{Z}X}^{-1}\frac{1}{n}\sum_{j\in[J]}\sum_{i\in I_{n,j}}\left[\widetilde{Z}_{i,j}\varepsilon_{i,j}-\widetilde{Z}_{i,j}X_{i,j}(\hat{\beta}^{r}_{tsls}-\beta_{n})\right]\right|

\displaystyle=\left|\widehat{Q}_{\widetilde{Z}X}^{-1}\frac{1}{n}\sum_{j\in[J]}\sum_{i\in I_{n,j}}\left[\widetilde{Z}_{i,j}\varepsilon_{i,j}-\widetilde{Z}_{i,j}X_{i,j}(\hat{\beta}^{r}_{tsls}-\beta_{n})-\widetilde{Z}_{i,j}W_{i,j}^{\top}(\hat{\gamma}^{r}_{tsls}-\gamma)\right]\right|

\displaystyle=\left|\widehat{Q}_{\widetilde{Z}X}^{-1}\frac{1}{n}\sum_{j\in[J]}\sum_{i\in I_{n,j}}\widetilde{Z}_{i,j}\hat{\varepsilon}^{r}_{i,j}\right|=\left|\widehat{Q}^{-1}_{\widetilde{Z}X}\right|AR_{n},

by
\beta_{0}=\hat{\beta}^{r}_{tsls}
,

\sum_{j\in[J]}\sum_{i\in I_{n,j}}\widetilde{Z}_{i,j}W_{i,j}^{\top}=0
,
and
\hat{\varepsilon}^{r}_{i,j}=\varepsilon_{i,j}-X_{i,j}^{\top}(\hat{\beta}^{r}_{tsls}-\beta_{n})-W_{i,j}^{\top}(\hat{\gamma}^{r}_{tsls}-\gamma)
.

In addition, for the bootstrap statistics we have

\displaystyle AR^{*}_{n}(g)=\left|\frac{1}{n}\sum_{j\in[J]}\sum_{i\in I_{n,j}}g_{j}\widetilde{Z}_{i,j}\bar{\varepsilon}^{r}_{i,j}\right|=\left|\frac{1}{n}\sum_{j\in[J]}\sum_{i\in I_{n,j}}g_{j}\widetilde{Z}_{i,j}\hat{\varepsilon}^{r}_{i,j}\right|

and whenever
\widehat{Q}_{\tilde{Z}X}\neq 0
,

\displaystyle T^{s*}_{n}(g)=\left|\widehat{Q}_{\widetilde{Z}X}^{-1}\frac{1}{n}\sum_{j\in[J]}\sum_{i\in I_{n,j}}g_{j}\widetilde{Z}_{i,j}\hat{\varepsilon}^{r}_{i,j}\right|=\left|\widehat{Q}_{\widetilde{Z}X}^{-1}\right|AR^{*}_{n}(g).

Therefore,
1\{T_{n}>\hat{c}^{s}_{n}(1-\alpha)\}
is equal to

1\{AR_{n}>\hat{c}_{AR,n}(1-\alpha)\}
whenever
\widehat{Q}_{\widetilde{Z}X}\neq 0
. We conclude that

\liminf_{n\rightarrow\infty}\mathbb{P}\left\{\phi_{n}^{s}=\phi_{n}^{ar}\right\}=1
because
\liminf_{n\rightarrow\infty}\mathbb{P}\left\{\widehat{Q}_{\widetilde{Z}X}\neq 0\right\}=1
.

\blacksquare

21
Proof of Theorem
19.2

The proof for the bootstrap LM test follows similar arguments as those for the studentized version of the bootstrap AR test.
Let
\mathbb{S}\equiv\textbf{R}^{d_{z}\times d_{x}}\times\otimes_{j\in[J]}\textbf{R}^{d_{z}}
,
and write an element
s\in\mathbb{S}
by
s=\left(\{s_{1,j}:j\in[J]\},\{s_{2,j}:j\in[J]\}\right)
.
We identify any
(g_{1},...,g_{q})=g\in\textbf{G}=\{-1,1\}^{J}
with an action on
s\in\mathbb{S}

given by
gs=\left(\{s_{1,j}:j\in[J]\},\{g_{j}s_{2,j}:j\in[J]\}\right)
.
We define the function
T_{LM}:\mathbb{S}\rightarrow\textbf{R}
to be given by

\displaystyle T_{LM}(s)\equiv\Bigg\|\left(D(s)^{\top}\left(\sum_{j\in[J]}s_{2,j}s^{\top}_{2,j}\right)^{-1}D(s)\right)^{-1/2}D(s)^{\top}\left(\sum_{j\in[J]}s_{2,j}s^{\top}_{2,j}\right)^{-1}\sum_{j\in[J]}s_{2,j}\Bigg\|^{2},

(73)

for any
s\in\mathbb{S}
such that
\sum_{j\in[J]}s_{2,j}s_{2,j}^{\top}
and

D(s)^{\top}\left(\sum_{j\in[J]}s_{2,j}s^{\top}_{2,j}\right)^{-1}D(s)
are invertible
and set
T_{LM}(s)=0
whenever one of the two is not invertible,
where

\displaystyle D(s)

\displaystyle\equiv\left(D_{1}(s),...,D_{d_{x}}(s)\right),

\displaystyle D_{l}(s)

\displaystyle\equiv\sum_{j\in[J]}s_{1,j,l}-\left(\sum_{j\in[J]}s_{1,j,l}s_{2,j}^{\top}\right)\left(\sum_{j\in[J]}s_{2,j}s_{2,j}^{\top}\right)^{-1}\sum_{j\in[J]}s_{2,j},

(74)

for
s_{1,j}=(s_{1,j,1},...,s_{1,j,d_{x}})
and
l=1,...,d_{x}
.

Furthermore, define the statistic
S_{n}
as

\displaystyle S_{n}\equiv\left(\left\{\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i\in I_{n,j}}\tilde{Z}_{i,j}X_{i,j}^{\top}:j\in[J]\right\},\left\{\frac{r_{n}}{n}\sum_{i\in I_{n,j}}\tilde{Z}_{i,j}\epsilon_{i,j}:j\in[J]\right\}\right),

(75)

Note that for
l=1,...,d_{x}
and
j\in[J]
, by Assumptions
1
(iii) and
2
(i) we have

\displaystyle\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i\in I_{n,j}}\tilde{Z}_{i,j}X_{i,j,l}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle p}}{{\longrightarrow}}\xi_{j}Q_{\tilde{Z}X,j,l},

(76)

where
Q_{\tilde{Z}X,j,l}
denotes the
l
-th column of the
d_{z}\times d_{x}
-dimensional matrix
Q_{\tilde{Z}X,j}
.
Then, by Assumptions
1
(ii) and
1
(iii),
3
(i) and
3
(ii),
and the continuous mapping theorem we have

\displaystyle S_{n}\xrightarrow{\hskip 4.46255ptd\hskip 4.46255pt}\left(\left\{\xi_{j}a_{j}Q_{\tilde{Z}X}:j\in[J]\right\},\left\{\mathcal{Z}_{j}:j\in[J]\right\}\right)\equiv S,

(77)

where
\xi_{j}>0
for all
j\in[J]
.
Also notice that for
l=1,...,d_{x}
,

\displaystyle\widehat{D}_{l}

\displaystyle=\sum_{j\in[J]}\left(\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i\in I_{n,j}}\tilde{Z}_{i,j}X_{i,j,l}\right)-\left(\sum_{j\in[J]}\left(\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i\in I_{n,j}}\tilde{Z}_{i,j}X_{i,j,l}\right)\left(\frac{r_{n}}{n}\sum_{k\in I_{k,j}}\tilde{Z}_{k,j}\bar{\epsilon}^{r}_{k,j}\right)^{\top}\right)

\displaystyle\cdot\left(\sum_{j\in[J]}\left(\frac{r_{n}}{n}\sum_{i\in I_{i,j}}\tilde{Z}_{i,j}\bar{\epsilon}^{r}_{i,j}\right)\left(\frac{r_{n}}{n}\sum_{k\in I_{k,j}}\tilde{Z}_{k,j}\bar{\epsilon}^{r}_{k,j}\right)^{\top}\right)^{-1}\frac{r_{n}}{n}\sum_{i\in I_{i,j}}\tilde{Z}_{i,j}\bar{\epsilon}^{r}_{i,j},

(78)

and
\frac{r_{n}}{n}\sum_{i\in I_{n,j}}\tilde{Z}_{i,j}\bar{\varepsilon}_{i,j}^{r}=\frac{r_{n}}{n}\sum_{i\in I_{n,j}}\tilde{Z}_{i,j}\varepsilon_{i,j}+o_{P}(1)

by
\beta_{n}=\beta_{0}
and Lemma
12.3
.
In addition, we set
A_{n}\in\textbf{R}
to equal

\displaystyle A_{n}\equiv I\left\{\text{$\widehat{D}$ is of full rank value and $\widehat{\Omega}$ is invertible}\right\},

(79)

and we have

\displaystyle\liminf_{n\rightarrow\infty}\mathbb{P}\{A_{n}=1\}=1,

(80)

which holds because
\left\{\mathcal{Z}_{j}:j\in[J]\right\}
are independent and continuously distributed
with covariance matrices that are of full rank,
and
Q_{\tilde{Z}X,j}
are of full column rank for all
j\in\mathcal{J}_{s}
,
by Assumptions
1
(ii),
3
(i), and
3
(ii).

It follows that whenever
A_{n}=1
,

\displaystyle\left(LM_{n},\{LM^{*}_{n}(g):g\in\textbf{G}\}\right)=\left(T_{LM}(S_{n}),\{T_{LM}(gS_{n}):g\in\textbf{G}\}\right)+o_{P}(1).

(81)

In what follows, we denote the ordered values of
\{T_{LM}(gs):g\in\textbf{G}\}
by

\displaystyle T^{(1)}_{LM}(s|\textbf{G})\leq...\leq T^{|\textbf{G}|}_{LM}(s|\textbf{G}).

(82)

Next, we have

\displaystyle\limsup_{n\rightarrow\infty}\mathbb{P}\left\{LM_{n}>\hat{c}_{LM,n}(1-\alpha)\right\}

\displaystyle\leq\limsup_{n\rightarrow\infty}\mathbb{P}\left\{LM_{n}\geq\hat{c}_{LM,n}(1-\alpha);A_{n}=1\right\}

\displaystyle\leq\mathbb{P}\left\{T_{LM}(S)\geq\inf\left\{u\in\textbf{R}:\frac{1}{|\textbf{G}|}\sum_{g\in\textbf{G}}I\{T_{LM}(gS)\leq u\}\geq 1-\alpha\right\}\right\},

(83)

where the final inequality follows from (
75
), (
77
), (
80
), (
81
), the continuous mapping theorem
and Portmanteau’s theorem.
Therefore, setting
k^{*}\equiv\lceil|\textbf{G}|(1-\alpha)\rceil
, we can obtain from (
21
) that

\displaystyle\limsup_{n\rightarrow\infty}\mathbb{P}\left\{LM_{n}>\hat{c}_{LM,n}(1-\alpha)\right\}

\displaystyle\leq\mathbb{P}\left\{T_{LM}(S)>T_{LM}^{(k^{*})}(S|\textbf{G})\right\}+\mathbb{P}\left\{T_{LM}(S)=T_{LM}^{(k^{*})}(S|\textbf{G})\right\}

\displaystyle\leq\alpha+\mathbb{P}\left\{T_{LM}(S)=T_{LM}^{(k^{*})}(S|\textbf{G})\right\},

(84)

where the final inequality follows by
gS\overset{d}{=}S
for all
g\in\textbf{G}
and the properties of randomization tests.
Then, we notice that for all
g\in\textbf{G}
,
T_{LM}(gS)=T_{LM}(-gS)
with probability 1, and
P\{T_{LM}(gS)=T_{LM}(\tilde{g}S)\}=0
for
\tilde{g}\notin\{g,-g\}
.
Therefore,

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\left\{T_{LM}(S)=T_{LM}^{(k^{*})}(S|\textbf{G})\right\}=\frac{1}{2^{J-1}}.

(85)

The claim of the upper bound in the theorem then follows from (
21
)
and (
85
).
The proof for the lower bound is similar to that for the bootstrap AR test, and thus is omitted.

To prove the result for the CQLR test, we note that

\displaystyle LR_{n}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\left\{AR_{CR,n}-rk_{n}+\sqrt{(AR_{CR,n}-rk_{n})^{2}+4\cdot LM_{n}\cdot rk_{n}}\right\}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\left\{AR_{CR,n}-rk_{n}+\left|AR_{CR,n}-rk_{n}\right|\sqrt{1+\frac{4\cdot LM_{n}\cdot rk_{n}}{(AR_{CR,n}-rk_{n})^{2}}}\right\}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\left\{AR_{CR,n}-rk_{n}+\left|AR_{CR,n}-rk_{n}\right|\left(1+2\cdot LM_{n}\frac{rk_{n}}{(AR_{CR,n}-rk_{n})^{2}}(1+o_{P}(1))\right)\right\}

\displaystyle=LM_{n}\frac{rk_{n}}{rk_{n}-AR_{CR,n}}(1+o_{P}(1))=LM_{n}+o_{P}(1),

(86)

where the third equality follows from the mean value expansion
\sqrt{1+x}=1+(1/2)(x+o(1))
,
the fourth and last equalities follow from
AR_{CR,n}-rk_{n}<0
w.p.a.1 since
AR_{CR,n}=O_{P}(1)
while
rk_{n}\rightarrow\infty
w.p.a.1 under
Assumption
3
(i)
.
Using arguments similar to those in (
21
), we obtain that for each
g\in\textbf{G}
,

\displaystyle LR^{*}_{n}(g)=LM^{*}_{n}(g)\frac{rk_{n}}{rk_{n}-AR^{*}_{CR,n}(g)}(1+o_{P}(1))=LM^{*}_{n}(g)+o_{P}(1),

(87)

by
AR^{*}_{CR,n}(g)-rk_{n}<0
w.p.a.1 since
AR^{*}_{CR,n}(g)=O_{P}(1)
for each
g\in\textbf{G}
.
Then, it follows that whenever
A_{n}=1
,

\displaystyle\left(LR_{n},\{LR^{*}_{n}(g):g\in\textbf{G}\}\right)=\left(T_{LM}(S_{n}),\{T_{LM}(gS_{n}):g\in\textbf{G}\}\right)+o_{P}(1).

(88)

Then, we obtain that

\displaystyle\limsup_{n\rightarrow\infty}\mathbb{P}\left\{LR_{n}>\hat{c}_{LR,n}(1-\alpha)\right\}

\displaystyle\leq\limsup_{n\rightarrow\infty}\mathbb{P}\left\{LR_{n}\geq\hat{c}_{LR,n}(1-\alpha);A_{n}=1\right\}

\displaystyle\leq\mathbb{P}\left\{T_{LM}(S)\geq\inf\left\{u\in\textbf{R}:\frac{1}{|\textbf{G}|}\sum_{g\in\textbf{G}}I\{T_{LM}(gS)\leq u\}\geq 1-\alpha\right\}\right\},

(89)

where the second inequality follows from (
75
), (
77
), (
80
), (
88
), the continuous mapping theorem and Portmanteau’s theorem.
Finally, the upper and lower bounds for the studentized bootstrap CQLR test follows from the previous arguments for the bootstrap LM test.

\blacksquare

Further Monte Carlo Simulation Results

22.1
Further Simulation Results for DGP 1

In this section, we report further simulation results for DGP 1. We notice that the overall pattern is very similar to that observed in Section
7
.

First, we report the results for DGP 1 with the number of clusters
J
equal to
9
or
20
. The size results with
d_{z}=1
and
d_{z}=3
are reported in Tables
8
and
9
, respectively.
In addition, we report the further power results in Figures
5
-
5
for
d_{z}=1
and
J=9
or
20
,
Figures
7
-
7
for
d_{z}=3
and
J=6
or
12
,
and Figures
9
-
9
for
d_{z}=3
and
J=9
or
20
, respectively. For the power results, we vary the value of
\beta_{0}-\beta
from
-3
to
3
with the true value of
\beta
equal to 1 throughout the simulations.
\rho_{\varepsilon v}
is set equal to
0.5
.

Second, we report the simulation results under DGP 1 for the case where
\Pi\in\{0.25,0.375,0.5\}
remains the same across clusters (i.e., homoskedastic
\Pi
), so that the cluster-level heterogeneity in identification strength originates solely from the heterogeneity in cluster size.
Other settings remain the same as those in DGP 1 in Section
7.1
.
Tables
10
-
13
report the size properties of the ten inference methods with
d_{z}=1
or
3
.
In addition,
Figures
11
-
13
report the power properties of the Wald and AR tests, respectively, with
d_{z}=1
.
Figures
15
-
17
report those for the Wald and AR tests, respectively, with
d_{z}=3
.

Furthermore, we investigate the relationship between the performance of alternative procedures and the number of clusters
J
. Specifically, we plot the size results as a function of
J
(from
J=6
to
30
) for
d_{z}=1
in Figure
18
and
d_{z}=3
in Figure
19
, respectively.
In order to handle the cases with relatively large values of
J
(e.g.,
J=30
), we let the cluster heterogeneity parameter
r
equal to 3 for
d_{z}=1
and 2.4 for
d_{z}=3
, respectively, so that inference methods that are based on cluster-level estimators (IM and CRS) can also be implemented even with
J=30
.
37
37

37

As the total number of observations
n
is equal to 500, the smallest and largest cluster sizes are 2 and 63 for
r=3
(4 and 56 for
r=2.4
), respectively, under
J=30
.

We focus on DGP 1 in
7.1
of the main text
with
\Pi=0.25
and
\rho_{\epsilon v}=0.5
throughout these simulations.

We observe that similar to the findings in previous simulations, IM and CRS tests can have considerable over-rejections when the number of clusters is relatively large, while ASY and BCH tests typically have large size distortions when the number of clusters is relatively small.
Among the seven Wald-based inference methods, only the three wild bootstrap tests (WRE, W-B, and W-B-S) have reasonable size control across different values of
J
. Although slightly lower than the nominal level when
J
is small, their null rejection frequencies tend to become rather close to
10\%
as the number of clusters becomes sufficiently large (e.g., when
J
is larger than 20).
We also notice that all the three AR-based inference methods (AR-ASY, AR-B, and AR-B-S) tend to control size. The two bootstrap AR tests over-reject slightly when
J
is small, while the asymptotic AR test (AR-ASY) can be rather conservative in the over-identified case (Figure
19
).

Finally, we investigate the power properties of alternative procedures as a function of
J
, from
J=6
to
30
. Similar to previous simulations, we focus on the power performance of the inference methods that have good size control (namely, WRE, W-B, W-B-S, AR-B, AR-B-S, and AR-ASY).
The true value of
\beta
is set equal to 1 throughout the simulations.
Figures
20
and
21
report the results for
d_{z}=1
with
\beta_{0}=3
and
-1
, respectively. In addition, Figures
22
and
23
report those for
d_{z}=3
with
\beta_{0}=3
and
-1
, respectively.
We highlight several observations below.
First, the power of all the six inference methods tends to increase with the number of clusters. Second, our recommended procedure W-B-S has the highest power among the six methods across different values of
J
. Third, among the AR tests, the unstudentized bootstrap AR test (AR-B) has
the best power performance while AR-ASY has the lowest power. The three observations are in line with our theoretical results in the main text.

22.3
Simulation Results for DGPs 3 and 4

In this section, we introduce two more simulation designs: DGPs 3 and 4.

DGP 3.
We consider a time series IV model.

\displaystyle y_{t}

\displaystyle=\gamma+\beta X_{t}+\varepsilon_{t},\;\varepsilon_{t}=\rho_{{}_{TS}}\varepsilon_{t-1}+u_{t},

\displaystyle X_{t}

\displaystyle=\Pi\iota_{d_{z}}^{\top}Z_{t}+v_{t},\;Z_{t}=\rho_{{}_{TS}}Z_{t-1}+w_{t},\;w_{t}\sim N(0,I_{d_{z}}),

\displaystyle\begin{pmatrix}u_{t}\\
v_{t}\end{pmatrix}

\displaystyle\sim N\left(\begin{pmatrix}0\\
0\end{pmatrix},\frac{1}{1-\rho^{2}_{{}_{TS}}}\begin{pmatrix}1&0.7\\
0.7&1\end{pmatrix}\right),

where
\iota_{d_{z}}
is a
d_{z}
-dimensional vector of ones.
We vary the IV strength
\Pi\in\{0.25,0.375,0.5\}
,
the number of IVs
d_{z}\in\{1,5\}
, and the AR(1) parameter
\rho_{{}_{TS}}\in\{0.3,0.5,0.7\}
. We let
\beta=1
and
\gamma=0
.
The number of observations is set at 160.
The simulation design is similar to the one for time series data considered by
Ibragimov and
Müller 2010
,
Bester
et al. 2011
, and
Canay
et al. 2017
.
We divide the data into
J
consecutive blocks (clusters) of equal size, where
J\in\{8,10,16\}
.

DGP 4.
We consider a spatial IV model.

\displaystyle y_{i}

\displaystyle=\gamma+\beta X_{i}+u_{i},\;X_{i}=\Pi\iota^{\top}_{d_{z}}Z_{i}+v_{i},

where
\beta=1
and
\gamma=0
.
The number of observations is 160 and the observations lie within a square of dimension
40\times 40
. The location for each observation
s_{i}=(s_{i_{1}},s_{i_{2}})
are drawn uniformly within the square, i.e.,
we let
s_{i_{1}}\sim U[0,40]
independently of
s_{i_{2}}\sim U[0,40]
.
The distance between observations at locations
s_{i}
and
s_{j}
is Euclidean:

d_{ij}=\sqrt{(s_{i_{1}}-s_{j_{1}})^{2}+(s_{i_{2}}-s_{j_{2}})^{2}}
.
The simulation design is similar to those considered in

Bester
et al. 2011
,
Sun and Kim 2015
, and
Lee and
Robinson 2016
. The errors
(u_{i},v_{i})^{\top}
are drawn from

N\left(\begin{pmatrix}0\\
0\end{pmatrix},\begin{pmatrix}1&0.7\\
0.7&1\end{pmatrix}\right)
.
In addition,
u_{i}
and
u_{j}
have a correlation equal to
\rho_{{}_{SP}}^{d_{ij}}
between them.
Similarly,
v_{i}
and
v_{j}
have a correlation equal to
\rho_{{}_{SP}}^{d_{ij}}
.
Furthermore, the IVs
Z_{i}
are drawn from
N(0,I_{d_{z}})
, with a correlation
\rho_{{}_{SP}}^{d_{ij}}
between each element of
Z_{i}
and its corresponding element of
Z_{j}
.
Therefore,
\rho_{SP}
controls the degree of spatial dependence and we let
\rho_{{}_{SP}}\in\{0.3,0.5,0.7\}
.
We vary the IV strength
\Pi\in\{0.25,0.375,0.5\}
and the number of IVs
d_{z}\in\{1,5\}
.
To implement inference, we split the data into
J
groups (clusters) with equal area, where
J\in\{6,9,12\}
.
38
38

38

Specifically, for
J=9
, we split the data into 9 groups formed by splitting into three equal parts both vertically and horizontally. For
J=6
, we split into two equal parts vertically and three horizontally, while for
J=12
, we split into three equal parts vertically and four horizontally.

Note: IM, CRS, ASY, BCH, WRE, W-B, W-B-S, AR-ASY, AR-B, and AR-B-S denote the ten inference methods described in Section

.

Results for DGP 3.

Tables
14
-
15
report the size results for the time series IV model with
d_{z}=1
and
d_{z}=5
, respectively.
We observe that the standard Wald test (ASY) does not control size when the number of clusters is small, especially in the over-identified case.
For example, when
d_{z}=5
,
J=8
, and
\Pi=0.25
, its null rejection frequencies are
0.183,0.196
, and
0.251
for
\rho_{TS}=0.3,0.5,
and
0.7
, respectively.
BCH improves upon ASY but still has null rejection frequencies equal to
0.135
,
0.143
, and
0.186
, respectively, under such settings.
On the other hand, IM and CRS typically have large size distortion when the number of clusters is large.
For example, when
d_{z}=5
,
J=16
, and
\Pi=0.25
, the null rejection frequencies for IM are

0.259,0.259,
and
0.249
, for
\rho_{TS}=0.3,0.5,
and
0.7
, respectively, while those for CRS are

0.267,0.266,
and
0.256
. By contrast, the wild bootstrap-based Wald inference methods (WRE, W-B, W-B-S) have good size control regardless of the number of clusters and the number of IVs. Therefore, we focus on the bootstrap methods when comparing power. Figures
28
and
29
report the power properties of the Wald and AR tests, respectively, with
d_{z}=1
. We find that the power curves are very similar to those in DGPs 1 and 2. Overall, our W-B-S has the best power, especially against distant alternatives.

Note: IM, CRS, ASY, BCH, WRE, W-B, W-B-S, AR-ASY, AR-B, and AR-B-S denote the ten inference methods described in Section

.

Results for DGP 4.

Tables
16
-
17
report the size for the spatial IV model with
d_{z}=1
and
d_{z}=5
, respectively.
The patterns are similar to those observed in other DGPs.
ASY and BCH tend to have relatively large size distortions
when the number of clusters
J
is small,
while IM and BCH tend to have large distortions when
J
is large.
In addition, the size distortions of these four inference methods increase when the number of IVs becomes large (
d_{z}=5
).
By contrast, WRE, W-B, and W-B-S have good size control across different settings.
Figures
30
and
31
report the power for the Wald and AR tests, respectively. Again, we see that overall W-B-S has the best power among these tests.
